Substantive amendment

5122-29-15 — Peer support services

Proposed (A)-(D) describes nonclinical peer support for individuals with mental illness and/or substance use disorder, potentially co-occurring developmental/intellectual disability, and their caregivers/families. It defines recovery, resiliency and wellness and lists 19 possible activities. Certified-peer delivery and disqualifying-event reporting remain in (H)-(I). Proposed (A) narrows eligibility: the current rule covers individuals with intellectual or developmental disabilities as a standalone population; the proposal requires a mental illness or substance use disorder (or both) and treats I/DD only as a co-occurring condition. Proposed (B) also newly labels the service non-clinical.

Likely impact: Agency peer-program templates and rendering-provider training should address individualized nonclinical support and family engagement, with certification and reporting controls retained.

Next action: Peer program lead/credentialing: crosswalk activities and certification status.

⚠️ Risk Flag. The broader DBH definition does not independently authorize Medicaid payment for every activity or family encounter. Proposed text carries forward the current rule's 'in accordance with in rule' in (H) and introduces 'employeed' in (I).

October 7 → October 9 revision: Corrects 'substance us disorder' to 'substance use disorder'; other changes are punctuation/spacing. Revised text introduces 'employeed' where the October 7 clean packet had 'employed'. No substantive October 7-to-revised service expansion identified. Revised PDF pages 35–37.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective April 7, 2022 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) Peer support services are services for individuals with a mental illness, intellectual or developmental disabilities, or substance use disorders and their caregivers and families.

(B) Peer support services consist of activities that promote resiliency and recovery, self-determination, advocacy, well-being, and skill development. Peer support services are individualized, resiliency and recovery focused, and based on increasing knowledge and skills through a peer relationship that supports an individual's or family's ability to address needs, navigate systems and promote recovery, resiliency, and wellness. They promote family driven, youth guided, trauma informed care and cultural humility, encourage partnership with individuals and families, and advocate for informed choice.

(C) For the purposes of this rule, the following definitions apply:

(1) "Recovery" means the personal process of change in which an individual strives to improve their health and wellness, resiliency, and reach their full potential through self-directed actions.

(2) "Resiliency" means the ability to recover from setbacks, adapt well to change, and keep going in the face of adversity. It is the process of adapting well in the face of adversity, trauma, tragedy, threats, or significant sources of stress such as family and relationship problems, serious health problems, or workplace and financial stressors.

(3) "Wellness " means a broad approach for things individuals can do at their own pace, in their own time, and within their own abilities, that can help them feel better and live longer.

(D) Peer support services may include, but are not limited to:

(1) Ongoing exploration of recovery, resiliency, and wellness needs;

(2) Supporting individuals and their caregivers and families in achieving goals through increased knowledge, skills and connection as identified by the individual or family;

(3) Encouraging hope;

(4) Supporting the development of life skills;

(5) Developing and working toward achievement of individualized recovery, resiliency, and wellness goals;

(6) Modeling personal responsibility for resiliency, recovery and wellness;

(7) Teaching and coaching skills to effectively navigate systems to effectively and efficiently utilize services;

(8) Addressing skills or behaviors, through processes that assist an individual, caregiver, or family in eliminating barriers to achieving or maintaining recovery, resiliency, and wellness;

(9) Assisting with accessing and developing natural support systems;

(10) Promoting coordination and linkage among providers;

(11) Coordinating or assisting in crisis interventions and stabilization;

(12) Conducting outreach and community education;

(13) Attending and participating in team decision making or specific treatment team; or,

(14) Assisting individuals, caregivers, or families in the development of empowerment skills through advocacy and activities that mitigate discrimination and inspire hope.

(E) Providing services in a culturally inclusive and competent manner which includes not practicing, condoning, facilitating, or collaborating in any form of discrimination on the basis of ethnicity, race, gender, sexual orientation, age, religion, national origin, marital status, political belief, or mental or physical disability.

(F) Peer support services are not site specific but shall be provided in locations that meet the needs of the individual, caregiver, or families.

(G) Peer support services may be facilitated to individuals, families, or groups.

(H) Peer support services shall be provided a person certified in accordance with in rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code.

(I) Peer support services providers shall report for any certified peer supporter employed by or volunteering with the provider to the Ohio department of mental health and addiction services any events that would disqualify the certified peer supporter pursuant to rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-15 Peer support services.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 35–37 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A) Peer support services are services for: (1) individuals with a mental illness
or substance use disorder, or both, who may also have
a co-occurring developmental or intellectual disability and (2) the caregivers and
families of such individuals.
(B) Peer support services consist of activities that promote resiliency and recovery, self-
determination, advocacy, well-being, and skill development. Peer support services are
individualized, resiliency and recovery focused, non-clinical, and based on increasing
knowledge and skills through a peer relationship that supports an individual's or family's
ability to address needs, navigate systems and promote recovery, resiliency, and wellness.
They promote family driven, youth guided, trauma informed care and cultural humility,
encourage partnership with individuals and families, and advocate for informed choice.
(C) As used in this rule:
(1) "Peer support services provider" means a provider, described in rule 5122-25-01 of
the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code
applies, that is certified under this rule to provide peer support services.
(2) "Recovery" means the personal process of change in which an individual strives to
improve their health and wellness, resiliency, and reach their full potential through
self-directed actions.
(3) "Resiliency" means the ability to recover from setbacks, adapt well to change, and
keep going in the face of adversity. It is the process of adapting well in the face of
adversity, trauma, tragedy, threats, or significant sources of stress such as family and
relationship problems, serious health problems, or workplace and financial stressors.
(4) "Wellness " means a broad approach for things individuals can do at their own
pace, in their own time, and within their own abilities, that can help them feel better
and live longer.
(D) Peer support services may include, but are not limited to:
(1) Ongoing exploration of recovery, resiliency, and wellness needs;
(2) Supporting individuals and their caregivers and families in achieving goals through
increased knowledge, skills and connection as identified by the individual or family;
(3) Encouraging hope, empowerment, and personal agency in the recovery process;
(4) Supporting the development of life skills and practical daily functioning;
(5) Developing and working toward individualized recovery, resiliency,
and wellness goals, while supporting multiple pathways;
(6) Modeling personal responsibility for resiliency, recovery and lived experience to
teach, mentor, and inspire recovery and wellness;
(7) Teaching and coaching skills to navigate behavioral health, social service,
and community systems to effectively and efficiently utilize services;
(8)
Assisting individuals, caregivers, and families in addressing and
eliminating barriers to achieve and maintain recovery
;
(9) Assisting with accessing , developing, and engaging natural supports,
community resources, and formal or informal support systems;
(10) Promoting coordination and linkage among providers, systems, and community
resources;
(11) Coordinating or assisting in crisis interventions and stabilization;
(12) Conducting outreach and community education to increase awareness and
engagement;
(13) Attending and participating in team decision making or treatment team
meetings;
(14) Planning, developing, and facilitating peer recovery group or peer-led recovery
activities;
(15) Creating and updating recovery plans;
(16) Providing mentoring, mutual support, and guidance based on lived experience;
(17) Assisting individuals to engage in behavioral health services and overcome barriers
to participation;
(18) Engaging additional supports that promote active participation in the individual's
recovery; and
(19) Assisting individuals, caregivers, or families in the development of
empowerment skills through advocacy and activities that mitigate discrimination
and inspire hope.
(E) Providing services in a culturally inclusive and competent manner which includes not
practicing, condoning, facilitating, or collaborating in any form of discrimination on the
basis of ethnicity, race, gender, sexual orientation, age, religion, national origin, marital
status, political belief, or mental or physical disability.
(F) Peer support services are not site specific but are to be provided in locations that meet
the needs of the individual, caregiver, or families.
(G) Peer support services may be facilitated to individuals, families, or groups.
(H) Peer support services are to be provided by a person certified in accordance with in rule
5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code.
(I) Peer support services providers are to report
to the department of behavioral health any events involving a certified
peer supporter (whether employeed or volunteering) that would disqualify
that certified peer supporter under rule 5122-29-15.1 of the
Administrative Code.

Substantive amendment

5122-29-16 — Peer run organization service

Certification requires recovery supports plus certified peer support delivered outside comprehensive treatment planning and outside Medicaid/private-insurance third-party reimbursement, alongside at least one additional activity. Public grants/contracts/appropriations do not constitute the prohibited reimbursement for this paragraph. Governance (51% lived-experience or caregiver board), published predictable hours, broad community access, mobile-unit and telehealth provisions are new requirements compared with the current rule and are unchanged from the October 7 packet.

Likely impact: Agencies should distinguish this peer-run organization certification model and its funding stream from separately certified reimbursable peer services.

Next action: Agency leadership/finance: map funding streams; credentialing: verify the certification model.

⚠️ Risk Flag. This rule does not justify treating all agency peer revenue as unavailable. Proposed (C)(5)(c) still says 'rather that limitation' instead of 'rather than limitation'.

October 7 → October 9 revision: Corrects 'peer run organization ed service' in (C) and changes Peer run to Peer-run in (B), plus well-being to wellbeing. No substantive service or funding change identified. Revised PDF pages 38–40.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective January 1, 2018 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) "Peer run organization" means any service or activity that is planned, developed, administered, delivered, and evaluated by persons, a majority of whom have a direct lived experience of a mental health or substance use disorder.

(B) "Recovery" as used in this rule has the same meaning as defined in paragraph (C) of rule 5122-29-15 of the Administrative Code.

(C) Peer run organizations include but are not limited to consumer operated services, recovery community organizations, peer drop-in centers, and club houses.

(D) Peer run organizations shall:

(1) Have a primary goal of enhancing the quantity and quality of support available to individuals seeking recovery from mental health or substance use disorders;

(2) Be grounded in three core principles: a recovery vision, authenticity of voice, and accountability to the recovery community;

(3) Promote the strategies of public awareness and education, personal empowerment, and peer based- and other recovery support services and activities which may include: peer recovery support, telephone recovery support services, all-recovery meetings, structured volunteer/work activities, groups, social activities, or wellness activities;

(4) Be responsive to the needs of individuals participating in services and be based on local needs as identified by the individuals participating in the service.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-16 Peer run organization service.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 38–40 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A) As used in this rule:
(1) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code
to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
(2) "Recovery" means the personal process of change in which an individual strives to
improve their health, wellness, and resiliency, as well as reach their full potential
through self-directed actions.
(B) Peer-run organization service is comprised of both activities specified in paragraph (C)(1) of
this rule and one or more activities specified in paragraph (C)(2) of this rule that are
delivered by individuals, a majority of whom have direct lived experience with mental
illness or a substance use disorder, that leverages the power of those individuals' shared
lived experiences to promote the behavioral health recovery and wellbeing of other
persons with mental illness or a substance use disorder.
(C) For a provider to be certified to provide peer run organization service, the provider is to
demonstrate all of the following:
(1) That it is able to provide both of the following activities of the peer run organization
service:
(a) Recovery supports as defined in section 5119.01 of the Revised Code, whether
occurring in an individual or group setting; and
(b) Peer support services provided by certified peer supporters, as defined in rule
5122-29-15 of the Administrative Code, that are not delivered as part of a
comprehensive individualized treatment plan described in rule 5122-27-03 or
reimbursed on a fee-for service or other third-party reimbursement basis
through medicaid or private insurance. Receipt by the provider of a grant,
contract, appropriation, or other public funding to support peer run
organization services does not constitute reimbursement for purposes of this
paragraph.
(2) That it is able to provide one or more of the following activities of the peer run
organization service:
(a) Consultation to a board of alcohol, drug addiction, and mental health services
and other community agencies regarding identifying unmet recovery needs
and potential solutions for fulfilling those needs;
(b) Recovery planning;
(c) Recovery meetings;
(d) Support groups;
(e) Links to resources;
(f) Skills-building groups;
(g) Increasing recovery supports workforce capacity through training, development,
and other activities;
(h) Harm reduction activities that may be supported with funding from the United
States substance abuse and mental health services administration (including
overdose prevention and response services) according to guidance from that
federal agency;
(i) Education;
(j) Socialization activities;
(k) Wellness activities;
(l) Activities involving art;
(m) Meals;
(n) Health and fitness classes;
(o) Structured volunteer and work activities;
(p) Recovery-focused outreach and advocacy activities; or
(q) Hosting of recovery celebrations.
(3) That either of the following is true:
(a) At least fifty-one per cent of the individual members of the provider's board of
directors or other governing body self-identify as having direct, lived
experience and are in recovery from a mental illness or substance use disorder;
or
(b) In the case of a provider whose primary mission is the delivery of family peer
support services or services focused on the caregiver experience, at least fifty-
one per cent of the individual members of the provider's board of directors or
other governing body self-identify as caregivers of an individual with mental
illness or a substance use disorder.
(4) A provider must establish, publish, and generally maintain predictable hours
appropriate to its service model and the needs of the community it serves.
(5) That the provider makes the service available to all individuals in the community with
mental illness or substance use disorder, not restricting the service to participants or
residents of any particular housing program, treatment program, service agency, or
residential facility, and that all of the following are the case:
(a) The provider's outreach and promotional materials clearly state that the activities
of the service are open to all community members;
(b) The provider has no referral, enrollment, or eligibility standards limiting access
to individuals connected to a specific housing program, treatment provider,
service agency, or residential facility; and
(c) The provider's policies and handbook do not impose restrictive eligibility
criteria tied to particular housing, treatment, or service providers. For purposes
of demonstrating compliance with this paragraph, a provider is to maintain
outreach materials or web site statements, internal policy documents, and
attendance or intake records that reflect broad community participation rather
that limitation of activities to a specific housing program, treatment provider,
service agency, or residential facility.
(D) In addition to having at least one physical site as required in chapter 5122-25, a provider of
peer run organization service may use one or more mobile units intended to meet the
needs of underserved populations.
(1) A mobile unit is to be a van, larger recreational vehicle, or other similar type of larger
motor vehicle that is equipped with enough space for at least two adults to meet
comfortably for the provision and receipt of peer support services. The van, larger
recreational vehicle, or similar type of larger motor vehicle is not to be owned or
rented by a member of the provider's staff, governing board, or advisory board. A
mobile unit is not to be a motorcycle or bicycle.
(2) A mobile unit is to possess a valid license plate. A copy of the unit's vehicle
registration is to be on file at the provider's physical location listed on the provider's
application filed under rule 5122-25-02, 5122-25-03, or 5122-25-04 of the
Administrative Code.
(E) A provider of peer run organization service that provides peer support services, as described
in paragraph (C)(1)(b) of this rule, may use telehealth for the delivery of the peer support
services in accordance with rule 5122-26-22 of the Administrative Code.

-(A) "Peer run organization" means any service or activity that is planned, developed, administered, delivered, and evaluated by persons, a majority of whom have a direct lived experience of a mental health or substance use disorder.
-(B) "Recovery" as used in this rule has the same meaning as defined in paragraph (C) of rule 5122-29-15 of the Administrative Code.
-(C) Peer run organizations include but are not limited to consumer operated services, recovery community organizations, peer drop-in centers, and club houses.
-(D) Peer run organizations shall:
-(1) Have a primary goal of enhancing the quantity and quality of support available to individuals seeking recovery from mental health or substance use disorders;
-(2) Be grounded in three core principles: a recovery vision, authenticity of voice, and accountability to the recovery community;
-(3) Promote the strategies of public awareness and education, personal empowerment, and peer based- and other recovery support services and activities which may include: peer recovery support, telephone recovery support services, all-recovery meetings, structured volunteer/work activities, groups, social activities, or wellness activities;
-(4) Be responsive to the needs of individuals participating in services and be based on local needs as identified by the individuals participating in the service.
+(A) As used in this rule:
+(1) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
+(2) "Recovery" means the personal process of change in which an individual strives to improve their health, wellness, and resiliency, as well as reach their full potential through self-directed actions.
+(B) Peer-run organization service is comprised of both activities specified in paragraph (C)(1) of this rule and one or more activities specified in paragraph (C)(2) of this rule that are delivered by individuals, a majority of whom have direct lived experience with mental illness or a substance use disorder, that leverages the power of those individuals' shared lived experiences to promote the behavioral health recovery and wellbeing of other persons with mental illness or a substance use disorder.
+(C) For a provider to be certified to provide peer run organization service, the provider is to demonstrate all of the following:
+(1) That it is able to provide both of the following activities of the peer run organization service:
+(a) Recovery supports as defined in section 5119.01 of the Revised Code, whether occurring in an individual or group setting; and
+(b) Peer support services provided by certified peer supporters, as defined in rule 5122-29-15 of the Administrative Code, that are not delivered as part of a comprehensive individualized treatment plan described in rule 5122-27-03 or reimbursed on a fee-for service or other third-party reimbursement basis through medicaid or private insurance. Receipt by the provider of a grant, contract, appropriation, or other public funding to support peer run organization services does not constitute reimbursement for purposes of this paragraph.
+(2) That it is able to provide one or more of the following activities of the peer run organization service:
+(a) Consultation to a board of alcohol, drug addiction, and mental health services and other community agencies regarding identifying unmet recovery needs and potential solutions for fulfilling those needs;
+(b) Recovery planning;
+(c) Recovery meetings;
+(d) Support groups;
+(e) Links to resources;
+(f) Skills-building groups;
+(g) Increasing recovery supports workforce capacity through training, development, and other activities;
+(h) Harm reduction activities that may be supported with funding from the United States substance abuse and mental health services administration (including overdose prevention and response services) according to guidance from that federal agency;
+(i) Education;
+(j) Socialization activities;
+(k) Wellness activities;
+(l) Activities involving art;
+(m) Meals;
+(n) Health and fitness classes;
+(o) Structured volunteer and work activities;
+(p) Recovery-focused outreach and advocacy activities; or
+(q) Hosting of recovery celebrations.
+(3) That either of the following is true:
+(a) At least fifty-one per cent of the individual members of the provider's board of directors or other governing body self-identify as having direct, lived experience and are in recovery from a mental illness or substance use disorder; or
+(b) In the case of a provider whose primary mission is the delivery of family peer support services or services focused on the caregiver experience, at least fifty- one per cent of the individual members of the provider's board of directors or other governing body self-identify as caregivers of an individual with mental illness or a substance use disorder.
+(4) A provider must establish, publish, and generally maintain predictable hours appropriate to its service model and the needs of the community it serves.
+(5) That the provider makes the service available to all individuals in the community with mental illness or substance use disorder, not restricting the service to participants or residents of any particular housing program, treatment program, service agency, or residential facility, and that all of the following are the case:
+(a) The provider's outreach and promotional materials clearly state that the activities of the service are open to all community members;
+(b) The provider has no referral, enrollment, or eligibility standards limiting access to individuals connected to a specific housing program, treatment provider, service agency, or residential facility; and
+(c) The provider's policies and handbook do not impose restrictive eligibility criteria tied to particular housing, treatment, or service providers. For purposes of demonstrating compliance with this paragraph, a provider is to maintain outreach materials or web site statements, internal policy documents, and attendance or intake records that reflect broad community participation rather that limitation of activities to a specific housing program, treatment provider, service agency, or residential facility.
+(D) In addition to having at least one physical site as required in chapter 5122-25, a provider of peer run organization service may use one or more mobile units intended to meet the needs of underserved populations.
+(1) A mobile unit is to be a van, larger recreational vehicle, or other similar type of larger motor vehicle that is equipped with enough space for at least two adults to meet comfortably for the provision and receipt of peer support services. The van, larger recreational vehicle, or similar type of larger motor vehicle is not to be owned or rented by a member of the provider's staff, governing board, or advisory board. A mobile unit is not to be a motorcycle or bicycle.
+(2) A mobile unit is to possess a valid license plate. A copy of the unit's vehicle registration is to be on file at the provider's physical location listed on the provider's application filed under rule 5122-25-02, 5122-25-03, or 5122-25-04 of the Administrative Code.
+(E) A provider of peer run organization service that provides peer support services, as described in paragraph (C)(1)(b) of this rule, may use telehealth for the delivery of the peer support services in accordance with rule 5122-26-22 of the Administrative Code.

Substantive amendment

5122-29-17 — Psychosocial rehabilitation service (PSR)

Change detail: Service replacement within amended rule

The body continues to define PSR with agency capacity for nine activity categories, individualized delivery linked to a comprehensive treatment plan, interdisciplinary staffing, designated case coordination, staff development and residential-service separation. Paragraph (D) allows a high-school-diploma practitioner with relevant training to perform only (C)(1)(b), (c), and (i); (E) allows contracting with another provider certified under this rule. The revised source heading says CPST although the body says PSR.

Likely impact: Agencies need an activity-by-rendering-provider-qualification crosswalk and review of subcontractors. The rule-number continuity and mistaken heading cannot support automatic code/revenue conversion.

Next action: Clinical/billing leads: crosswalk PSR activities and member needs; credentialing: map qualification tiers and certified subcontractors; policy lead: request correction of the CPST heading and repeated paragraphs.

⚠️ Risk Flag. The source itself contains CPST-versus-PSR title/body inconsistency (p41) and repeats paragraphs (D) and (E): one occurrence is embedded in (C)(5)(c) on p43, and another begins at the left margin across pp43-44. These are real PDF defects, not extraction errors. Only three activity categories receive the express high-school/training permission; certification capacity does not equal every rendering provider's eligibility.

October 7 → October 9 revision: Heading changes from Psychosocial rehabilitation service (PSR) to Community psychiatric supportive treatment (CPST) service while body remains PSR. Adds a second identical (D)/(E) occurrence; 'real-world' spacing changes only. No expansion of the existing three-activity high-school permission or certified-provider contracting pathway identified. Revised PDF pages 41–44.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective September 16, 2018 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) Community psychiatric supportive treatment (CPST) service provides an array of services delivered by community based, mobile individuals or multidisciplinary teams of professionals and trained others. Services address the individualized mental health needs of the client. They are directed towards adults, children, adolescents and families and will vary with respect to hours, type and intensity of services, depending on the changing needs of each individual. The purpose/intent of CPST services is to provide specific, measurable, and individualized services to each person served. CPST services should be focused on the individual's ability to succeed in the community; to identify and access needed services; and to show improvement in school, work and family and integration and contributions within the community.

(B) Activities of the CPST service shall consist of one or more of the following:

(1) Ongoing assessment of needs;

(2) Assistance in achieving personal independence in managing basic needs as identified by the individual and/or parent or guardian;

(3) Facilitation of further development of daily living skills, if identified by the individual and/or parent or guardian;

(4) Coordination of the ISP, including:

(a) Services identified in the ISP;

(b) Assistance with accessing natural support systems in the community; and

(c) Linkages to formal community service/systems;

(5) Symptom monitoring;

(6) Coordination and/or assistance in crisis management and stabilization as needed;

(7) Advocacy and outreach;

(8) As appropriate to the care provided to individuals, and when appropriate, to the family, education and training specific to the individual's assessed needs, abilities and readiness to learn;

(9) Mental health interventions that address symptoms, behaviors, thought processes, etc., that assist an individual in eliminating barriers to seeking or maintaining education and employment; and

(10) Activities that increase the individual's capacity to positively impact his/her own environment.

(C) The methods of CPST service delivery shall consist of:

(1) Service delivery to the person served and/or any other individual who will assist in the person's mental health treatment.

(a) Service delivery may be face-to-face, by telephone, and/or by video conferencing; and

(b) Service delivery may be to individuals or groups.

(2) CPST services are not site specific. However, they must be provided in locations that meet the needs of the persons served. When a person served is enrolled in a residential treatment or residential support facility setting, CPST services must be provided by staff that are organized and distinct and separate from the residential service as evidenced by staff job descriptions, time allocation or schedules, and development of service rates.

(D) There must be one CPST staff who is clearly responsible for case coordination. This staff person must be an employee of an agency that is certified by ODMH to provide CPST services. This person may delegate CPST services to eligible providers internal and/or external to the certified agency as long as the following requirements and/or conditions are met:

(1) All delegated CPST activities are consistent with this rule in its entirety;

(2) The delegated CPST services may be provided by an entity not certified by ODMH to provide CPST services as long as there is written agreement between the certified agency and the non-certified entity that defines the service expectations, qualifications of staff, program and financial accountability, health and safety requirements, and required documentation; and

(3) An entity that is not certified by ODMH for CPST service may only seek reimbursement for CPST services through a certified agency and with a written agreement as required in this paragraph.

(E) Providers of CPST service shall have a staff development plan based upon identified individual needs of CPST staff. Evidence that the plan is being followed shall be maintained. The plan shall address, at a minimum, the following:

(1) An understanding of systems of care, such as natural support systems, entitlements and benefits, inter- and intra-agency systems of care, crisis response systems and their purpose, and the intent and activities of CPST;

(2) Characteristics of the population to be served, such as psychiatric symptoms, medications, culture, and age/gender development; and

(3) Knowledge of CPST purpose, intent and activities.

(F) Community psychiatric supportive treatment (CPST) service shall be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-17 Community psychiatric supportive treatment (CPST) service.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 41–44 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A) As used in this rule:
(1) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code
to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
(2) "Collateral contact" occurs when the provider contacts individuals who play a
significant role in the service recipient's life.
(B) Psychosocial rehabilitation (PSR) service
Psychosocial rehabilitation (PSR) service is a service comprised of one or more of the
mental health activities specified in paragraph (C) of this rule. For each individual who
receives the service, the service is to be tailored to that individual's needs consistent with
their comprehensive individualized treatment plan developed under rule 5122-27-03 of the
Administrative Code.
The service is intended for individuals with a mental health diagnosis resulting in
functional deficits who need intensive community support to maintain stability and
achieve recovery goals. It is intended to restore, rehabilitate, and support activities and
routines of daily functioning within the individual's natural community environment by
addressing impairments and barriers arising from and impacting the individual's mental
illness. The service may be delivered at a fixed site, an individual's home, or in the
community; the place of services delivery is to be based on the needs of the particular
individual served.
(C) For a provider to be certified to provide PSR service, the provider is to demonstrate all of
the following:
(1) That it is able to provide all of the following activities of the service:
(a) Ongoing assessment of needs The provider is able to complete regular
assessments of an individual's needs and barriers and, after each assessment,
review the individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan and
document review results in accordance with rule 5122-27-03 of the
Administrative Code.
(b) Assistance in achieving personal independence in managing basic needs The
provider is able to support an individual with independently managing their
identified basic needs and life skills directly related to their mental illness,
with progress measured by timeframes determined in the individual's
comprehensive individualized treatment plan that reflect the individual's
mental health-related functional goals.
(c) Facilitation of further development of daily living skills The provider is able to
facilitate development of an individual's identified daily living skills, directly
related to the individual's mental illness, through structured practice and
coaching until the individual meets the comprehensive individualized
treatment plan goals that reflect their mental health-related functional needs.
(d) Coordination of the comprehensive individualized treatment plan The provider
is able to coordinate implementation of an individual's comprehensive
individualized treatment plan by doing all of the following:
(i) Ensuring that services identified in the plan are initiated and maintained by
completing collateral contact and documenting the individual's
engagement and progress;
(ii) Assisting with the identification of and engagement with an individual's
natural supports, such as family and peers; and
(iii) Establishing linkages to formal community services/systems through
collateral contact identified in an individual's comprehensive
individualized treatment plan.
(e) Symptom monitoring The provider is able to monitor the individual's psychiatric
and/or substance use symptoms, review trends, and adjust coping strategies
and comprehensive individualized treatment plan interventions to achieve
stabilization within the community.
(f) Coordination and/or assistance in crisis management and stabilization as needed
Through collateral contact and direct service, the provider is able to coordinate
crisis management and stabilization of an individual by implementing a safety
plan, contacting crisis resources, and conducting post-crisis follow-up with the
individual to reduce crisis frequency and severity and maintain community
safety.
(g) Education and training The provider is able to deliver tailored psychoeducation
and skills training on identified topics and verify learning through
demonstrated skills and real-world application linked to comprehensive
individualized treatment plan goals.
(h) Mental health interventions The provider is able to deliver targeted mental
health interventions to reduce symptoms, behaviors, and thought patterns that
interfere with achieving comprehensive individualized treatment goals.
Intervention progress may be tracked by attendance, retention, symptom
reduction, and/or functional gains.
(i) Positive impact activities The provider is able to use positive impact and
community functioning interventions such as, but not limited to,
individualized education, modeling, role-play, community-based practice,
problem-solving coaching, and prompting to help the individual recognize
how their mental illness affects their community functioning, select and
implement appropriate settings, initiate pro-social actions, and evaluate the
outcome of each intervention as identified in the comprehensive
individualized treatment plan.
(2) That it is able to deliver the service to groups or individuals;
(3) That it is able to deliver the service face-to-face, by telephone, or by video
conferencing;
(4) That regarding staffing, it meets all of the following criteria:
(a) Subject to paragraph (D) of this rule, has an interdisciplinary team of staff
qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code to provide
and supervise the service;
(b) Ensures that each particular staff member on the interdisciplinary team provides
only those components of the service for which the individual possesses the
appropriate qualifications or credential;
(c) Ensures that when an individual served is enrolled in residential treatment or
residential support facility setting, the service is provided by staff who are
organized, distinct, and separate from the residential service as evidenced by
staff job descriptions, time allocations or schedules, and development of
service rates; and
(d) Ensures that each individual to be served is assigned one staff member, based on
the specific needs of the individual, who is the designated person to provide
case coordination.
(5) That it has a staff development plan that is based on identified individual needs of
staff providing the PSR service. Evidence that the plan is being followed is to be
maintained. The plan is to address, at a minimum, all of the following:
(a) An understanding of systems of care, such as natural support systems,
entitlements and benefits, inter-agency and intra-agency systems of care, crisis
response systems and their purpose, and the intent and activities of the service;
(b) Characteristics of the population to be served, such as psychiatric symptoms,
medications, culture, and age/gender development; and
(c) Knowledge of the service's purpose, intent, and activities. (D) A practitioner
who possesses a high school diploma and specific training related to persons
with mental health conditions or needs may perform the activities of the PSR
service specified in paragraphs (C)(1)(b), (C)(1)(c), and (C)(1)(i) of this rule.
(E) While, pursuant to paragraph (C)(1) of this rule, a provider is to
demonstrate the ability to provide all activities of the PSR service to be
certified for the service, a provider may contract with another provider
certified under this rule to provide one or more activities of the service
specified in that paragraph.
(D) A practitioner who possesses a high school diploma and specific training related to persons
with mental health conditions or needs may perform the activities of the PSR service
specified in paragraphs (C)(1)(b), (C)(1)(c), and (C)(1)(i) of this rule.
(E) While, pursuant to paragraph (C)(1) of this rule, a provider is to demonstrate the ability to
provide all activities of the PSR service to be certified for the service, a provider may
contract with another provider certified under this rule to provide one or more activities of
the service specified in that paragraph.
Current effective rule → revised proposal text diff

Paragraphs reflowed for comparison; source spelling and repeated provisions are retained. This diff compares the operative rule with the retained proposed text, excluding deleted redline text.

--- Current effective 5122-29-17
+++ October 9 revised 5122-29-17
@@ -1,29 +1,31 @@
-(A) Community psychiatric supportive treatment (CPST) service provides an array of services delivered by community based, mobile individuals or multidisciplinary teams of professionals and trained others. Services address the individualized mental health needs of the client. They are directed towards adults, children, adolescents and families and will vary with respect to hours, type and intensity of services, depending on the changing needs of each individual. The purpose/intent of CPST services is to provide specific, measurable, and individualized services to each person served. CPST services should be focused on the individual's ability to succeed in the community; to identify and access needed services; and to show improvement in school, work and family and integration and contributions within the community.
-(B) Activities of the CPST service shall consist of one or more of the following:
-(1) Ongoing assessment of needs;
-(2) Assistance in achieving personal independence in managing basic needs as identified by the individual and/or parent or guardian;
-(3) Facilitation of further development of daily living skills, if identified by the individual and/or parent or guardian;
-(4) Coordination of the ISP, including:
-(a) Services identified in the ISP;
-(b) Assistance with accessing natural support systems in the community; and
-(c) Linkages to formal community service/systems;
-(5) Symptom monitoring;
-(6) Coordination and/or assistance in crisis management and stabilization as needed;
-(7) Advocacy and outreach;
-(8) As appropriate to the care provided to individuals, and when appropriate, to the family, education and training specific to the individual's assessed needs, abilities and readiness to learn;
-(9) Mental health interventions that address symptoms, behaviors, thought processes, etc., that assist an individual in eliminating barriers to seeking or maintaining education and employment; and
-(10) Activities that increase the individual's capacity to positively impact his/her own environment.
-(C) The methods of CPST service delivery shall consist of:
-(1) Service delivery to the person served and/or any other individual who will assist in the person's mental health treatment.
-(a) Service delivery may be face-to-face, by telephone, and/or by video conferencing; and
-(b) Service delivery may be to individuals or groups.
-(2) CPST services are not site specific. However, they must be provided in locations that meet the needs of the persons served. When a person served is enrolled in a residential treatment or residential support facility setting, CPST services must be provided by staff that are organized and distinct and separate from the residential service as evidenced by staff job descriptions, time allocation or schedules, and development of service rates.
-(D) There must be one CPST staff who is clearly responsible for case coordination. This staff person must be an employee of an agency that is certified by ODMH to provide CPST services. This person may delegate CPST services to eligible providers internal and/or external to the certified agency as long as the following requirements and/or conditions are met:
-(1) All delegated CPST activities are consistent with this rule in its entirety;
-(2) The delegated CPST services may be provided by an entity not certified by ODMH to provide CPST services as long as there is written agreement between the certified agency and the non-certified entity that defines the service expectations, qualifications of staff, program and financial accountability, health and safety requirements, and required documentation; and
-(3) An entity that is not certified by ODMH for CPST service may only seek reimbursement for CPST services through a certified agency and with a written agreement as required in this paragraph.
-(E) Providers of CPST service shall have a staff development plan based upon identified individual needs of CPST staff. Evidence that the plan is being followed shall be maintained. The plan shall address, at a minimum, the following:
-(1) An understanding of systems of care, such as natural support systems, entitlements and benefits, inter- and intra-agency systems of care, crisis response systems and their purpose, and the intent and activities of CPST;
-(2) Characteristics of the population to be served, such as psychiatric symptoms, medications, culture, and age/gender development; and
-(3) Knowledge of CPST purpose, intent and activities.
-(F) Community psychiatric supportive treatment (CPST) service shall be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
+(A) As used in this rule:
+(1) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
+(2) "Collateral contact" occurs when the provider contacts individuals who play a significant role in the service recipient's life.
+(B) Psychosocial rehabilitation (PSR) service Psychosocial rehabilitation (PSR) service is a service comprised of one or more of the mental health activities specified in paragraph (C) of this rule. For each individual who receives the service, the service is to be tailored to that individual's needs consistent with their comprehensive individualized treatment plan developed under rule 5122-27-03 of the Administrative Code. The service is intended for individuals with a mental health diagnosis resulting in functional deficits who need intensive community support to maintain stability and achieve recovery goals. It is intended to restore, rehabilitate, and support activities and routines of daily functioning within the individual's natural community environment by addressing impairments and barriers arising from and impacting the individual's mental illness. The service may be delivered at a fixed site, an individual's home, or in the community; the place of services delivery is to be based on the needs of the particular individual served.
+(C) For a provider to be certified to provide PSR service, the provider is to demonstrate all of the following:
+(1) That it is able to provide all of the following activities of the service:
+(a) Ongoing assessment of needs The provider is able to complete regular assessments of an individual's needs and barriers and, after each assessment, review the individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan and document review results in accordance with rule 5122-27-03 of the Administrative Code.
+(b) Assistance in achieving personal independence in managing basic needs The provider is able to support an individual with independently managing their identified basic needs and life skills directly related to their mental illness, with progress measured by timeframes determined in the individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan that reflect the individual's mental health-related functional goals.
+(c) Facilitation of further development of daily living skills The provider is able to facilitate development of an individual's identified daily living skills, directly related to the individual's mental illness, through structured practice and coaching until the individual meets the comprehensive individualized treatment plan goals that reflect their mental health-related functional needs.
+(d) Coordination of the comprehensive individualized treatment plan The provider is able to coordinate implementation of an individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan by doing all of the following:
+(i) Ensuring that services identified in the plan are initiated and maintained by completing collateral contact and documenting the individual's engagement and progress;
+(ii) Assisting with the identification of and engagement with an individual's natural supports, such as family and peers; and (iii) Establishing linkages to formal community services/systems through collateral contact identified in an individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan.
+(e) Symptom monitoring The provider is able to monitor the individual's psychiatric and/or substance use symptoms, review trends, and adjust coping strategies and comprehensive individualized treatment plan interventions to achieve stabilization within the community.
+(f) Coordination and/or assistance in crisis management and stabilization as needed Through collateral contact and direct service, the provider is able to coordinate crisis management and stabilization of an individual by implementing a safety plan, contacting crisis resources, and conducting post-crisis follow-up with the individual to reduce crisis frequency and severity and maintain community safety.
+(g) Education and training The provider is able to deliver tailored psychoeducation and skills training on identified topics and verify learning through demonstrated skills and real-world application linked to comprehensive individualized treatment plan goals.
+(h) Mental health interventions The provider is able to deliver targeted mental health interventions to reduce symptoms, behaviors, and thought patterns that interfere with achieving comprehensive individualized treatment goals. Intervention progress may be tracked by attendance, retention, symptom reduction, and/or functional gains.
+(i) Positive impact activities The provider is able to use positive impact and community functioning interventions such as, but not limited to, individualized education, modeling, role-play, community-based practice, problem-solving coaching, and prompting to help the individual recognize how their mental illness affects their community functioning, select and implement appropriate settings, initiate pro-social actions, and evaluate the outcome of each intervention as identified in the comprehensive individualized treatment plan.
+(2) That it is able to deliver the service to groups or individuals;
+(3) That it is able to deliver the service face-to-face, by telephone, or by video conferencing;
+(4) That regarding staffing, it meets all of the following criteria:
+(a) Subject to paragraph (D) of this rule, has an interdisciplinary team of staff qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code to provide and supervise the service;
+(b) Ensures that each particular staff member on the interdisciplinary team provides only those components of the service for which the individual possesses the appropriate qualifications or credential;
+(c) Ensures that when an individual served is enrolled in residential treatment or residential support facility setting, the service is provided by staff who are organized, distinct, and separate from the residential service as evidenced by staff job descriptions, time allocations or schedules, and development of service rates; and
+(d) Ensures that each individual to be served is assigned one staff member, based on the specific needs of the individual, who is the designated person to provide case coordination.
+(5) That it has a staff development plan that is based on identified individual needs of staff providing the PSR service. Evidence that the plan is being followed is to be maintained. The plan is to address, at a minimum, all of the following:
+(a) An understanding of systems of care, such as natural support systems, entitlements and benefits, inter-agency and intra-agency systems of care, crisis response systems and their purpose, and the intent and activities of the service;
+(b) Characteristics of the population to be served, such as psychiatric symptoms, medications, culture, and age/gender development; and
+(c) Knowledge of the service's purpose, intent, and activities. (D) A practitioner who possesses a high school diploma and specific training related to persons with mental health conditions or needs may perform the activities of the PSR service specified in paragraphs (C)(1)(b), (C)(1)(c), and (C)(1)(i) of this rule.
+(E) While, pursuant to paragraph (C)(1) of this rule, a provider is to demonstrate the ability to provide all activities of the PSR service to be certified for the service, a provider may contract with another provider certified under this rule to provide one or more activities of the service specified in that paragraph.
+(D) A practitioner who possesses a high school diploma and specific training related to persons with mental health conditions or needs may perform the activities of the PSR service specified in paragraphs (C)(1)(b), (C)(1)(c), and (C)(1)(i) of this rule.
+(E) While, pursuant to paragraph (C)(1) of this rule, a provider is to demonstrate the ability to provide all activities of the PSR service to be certified for the service, a provider may contract with another provider certified under this rule to provide one or more activities of the service specified in that paragraph.

Substantive amendment

5122-29-18 — Therapeutic behavioral services

Change detail: Substantive service redesign

Proposed TBS is intensive one-on-one or group outpatient support for individuals with a mental health diagnosis, delivered short-term to address high-risk, disruptive or self-destructive behavior through behavioral/emotional management skills and comprehensive treatment-plan goals. It retains five activity categories, ongoing symptom/function monitoring, and either degree-qualified behavioral health practitioners or QBHS under 5122-29-30.

Likely impact: The October 7 adults-only concern is resolved in this DBH text. Agencies still need to evaluate clinical purpose, long-duration service assumptions, documentation and rendering-provider qualifications. Staffing alignment with ODM remains a separate question.

Next action: Credentialing: map each TBS practitioner against DBH degree/QBHS requirements and ODM degree/experience requirements. Clinical leads: review short-term clinical purpose, activity documentation and supervision without assuming youth are excluded.

⚠️ Risk Flag. DBH (C)(3) still uses bachelor's/master's practitioners or QBHS; ODM's supplied October draft 5160-27-08(C)(2) separately retains degree or high-school-plus-three-years experience for licensed/unlicensed practitioners. The team's crosswalk review verified that current 5122-29-30(D)'s QBHS route does not require a degree or a three-year minimum, so the change from an express high-school-plus-three-years route to QBHS wording does not categorically exclude non-degree staff. Keep certification qualifications and Medicaid reimbursement qualifications distinct. No numerical short-term duration or fixed TBS group ratio is added by this DBH revision.

October 7 → October 9 revision: (B) changes adults to individuals. (C)(1)(d) heading changes from 'Restoration of self-management skills social skills and daily functioning' to 'Restoration of self-management skills'. Body of that activity remains; (C)(2) explicitly retains social-skills/daily-function monitoring. Definition lead-in punctuation also changes. Revised PDF pages 45–46.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective January 1, 2018 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) Therapeutic behavioral services (TBS) and psychosocial rehabilitation (PSR) services are an array of activities intended to provide individualized supports or care coordination of healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non-healthcare services. TBS and PSR may involve collateral contacts and may be delivered in all settings that meet the needs of the individual.

(B) Service activities.

(1) TBS service activities include, but are not limited to the following:

(a) Consultation with a licensed practitioner or an elgible provider pursuant to paragraph (C) of this rule, to assist with the individual's needs and service planning for individualized supports or care coordination of healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non-healthcare services and development of a treatment plan;

(b) Referral and linkage to other healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non-healthcare services to avoid more restrictive levels of treatment;

(c) Interventions using evidence-based techniques;

(d) Identification of strategies or treatment options;

(e) Restoration of social skills and daily functioning; and,

(f) Crisis prevention and amelioration.

(2) PSR service activities include, but are not limited to the following

(a) Restoration, rehabilitation and support of daily functioning to improve self-management of the negative effects of psychiatric or emotional symptoms that interfere with a person's daily functioning;

(b) Restoration and implementation of daily functioning and daily routines critical to remaining successfully in home, school, work, and community; and,

(c) Rehabilitation and support to restore skills to function in a natural community environment.

(C) Eligible providers.

(1) Eligible providers of TBS are those practitioners who have :

(a) A bachelor's or master's degree in social work, psychology, nursing, or in related human services, or

(b) A high school diploma with a minimum of three years of relevant experience.

(2) Eligible providers for PSR services are those practitioners who have a high school diploma and specific training related to persons with mental health conditions or needs.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-18 Therapeutic behavioral services.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 45–46 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A) As used in this rule:
(1) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code
to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
(2) "Collateral contact" occurs when the provider contacts individuals who play a
significant role in the service recipient's life.
(B) Therapeutic behavioral services are intensive, one-on-one or group outpatient services for
individuals with a mental health diagnosis. They are supports and solution-focused
interventions, delivered on a short-term basis, intended to achieve identified goals or
objectives in an individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan developed
under rule 5122-27-03 of the Administrative Code and focused on reducing high-risk,
disruptive, and/or self-destructive behaviors by building specific behavioral and emotional
management skills. The service may be delivered in all settings that meet the needs of the
individual.
(C) For a provider to be certified to provide therapeutic behavioral services, the provider is to
demonstrate all of the following:
(1) That it is able to provide all of the following activities of therapeutic behavioral
services:
(a) Collateral contact
The provider is able to do both of the following:
(i) Consult with one or more eligible providers of the services, as specified in
paragraph (C)(3) of this rule, for the purpose of addressing an
individual's identified needs and participating in service planning. Such
consultation may include planning for individualized supports and
coordinating healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non healthcare
services in accordance with the individual's comprehensive
individualized treatment plan; and
(ii) Refer an individual to other healthcare, behavioral healthcare and non-
healthcare services to avoid more restrictive levels of treatment.
(b) Use of evidence-based techniques
The provider is able to provide solution-focused interventions and emotional
and behavioral management for an individual, drawn from evidence-based
modalities to ameliorate targeted symptoms and improve the individual's
capacity to cope with or prevent symptom manifestation.
(c) Treatment planning
With the individual's participation and consent, the provider is able to utilize
strengths-based treatment planning including assisting the individual or
individual's support systems (e.g., a parent, guardian, or other treatment
professionals involved with the individual's care) in identifying needs and
treatment goals associated with the individual's mental illness or behavioral
health condition.
(d) Restoration of self-management skills
The provider is able to provide therapeutic interventions designed to promote
safe and adaptive alternatives to high-risk, disruptive, and/or self-destructive
behaviors resulting in improved emotional and behavioral self-regulation and
functioning; improved insight and self-awareness; increased community
tenure; and effective functioning to support the individual's ability to maintain
safety within the individual's natural environment as related to their diagnosed
mental illness or behavioral health condition.
(e) Crisis prevention and amelioration
The provider is able to provide therapeutic support to assist an individual and
the individual's support systems to identify potential psychiatric or personal
crises, develop crisis management plans, and, as appropriate, provide
collateral contact to restore stability and functioning to avoid more restrictive
levels of treatment.
(2) That for each individual to receive the services, it is able to monitor reductions or
increases in symptom severity, the need for transition to a higher or lower level of
care, and increases or decreases in social skills and daily functioning. Monitoring is
needed to detect early signs of clinical deterioration, initiate timely intervention,
evaluate response to treatment, adjust the comprehensive individualized treatment
plan, and support the individual's recovery goals by strengthening coping skills,
community participation, and independence in daily living.
(3) That it employs or contracts with either or both of the following to provide the
services:
(a) One or more behavioral health practitioners who possess, at a minimum, a
bachelor's or master's degree in social work, psychology, nursing, or in a
related human services discipline; or
(b) One or more qualified behavioral health specialists, as defined in rule 5122-29-
30 of the Administrative Code.

Paragraphs reflowed for comparison; source spelling and repeated provisions are retained. This diff compares the operative rule with the retained proposed text, excluding deleted redline text.

-(A) Therapeutic behavioral services (TBS) and psychosocial rehabilitation (PSR) services are an array of activities intended to provide individualized supports or care coordination of healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non-healthcare services. TBS and PSR may involve collateral contacts and may be delivered in all settings that meet the needs of the individual.
-(B) Service activities.
-(1) TBS service activities include, but are not limited to the following:
-(a) Consultation with a licensed practitioner or an elgible provider pursuant to paragraph (C) of this rule, to assist with the individual's needs and service planning for individualized supports or care coordination of healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non-healthcare services and development of a treatment plan;
-(b) Referral and linkage to other healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non-healthcare services to avoid more restrictive levels of treatment;
-(c) Interventions using evidence-based techniques;
-(d) Identification of strategies or treatment options;
-(e) Restoration of social skills and daily functioning; and,
-(f) Crisis prevention and amelioration.
-(2) PSR service activities include, but are not limited to the following
-(a) Restoration, rehabilitation and support of daily functioning to improve self-management of the negative effects of psychiatric or emotional symptoms that interfere with a person's daily functioning;
-(b) Restoration and implementation of daily functioning and daily routines critical to remaining successfully in home, school, work, and community; and,
-(c) Rehabilitation and support to restore skills to function in a natural community environment.
-(C) Eligible providers.
-(1) Eligible providers of TBS are those practitioners who have :
-(a) A bachelor's or master's degree in social work, psychology, nursing, or in related human services, or
-(b) A high school diploma with a minimum of three years of relevant experience.
-(2) Eligible providers for PSR services are those practitioners who have a high school diploma and specific training related to persons with mental health conditions or needs.
+(A) As used in this rule:
+(1) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
+(2) "Collateral contact" occurs when the provider contacts individuals who play a significant role in the service recipient's life.
+(B) Therapeutic behavioral services are intensive, one-on-one or group outpatient services for individuals with a mental health diagnosis. They are supports and solution-focused interventions, delivered on a short-term basis, intended to achieve identified goals or objectives in an individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan developed under rule 5122-27-03 of the Administrative Code and focused on reducing high-risk, disruptive, and/or self-destructive behaviors by building specific behavioral and emotional management skills. The service may be delivered in all settings that meet the needs of the individual.
+(C) For a provider to be certified to provide therapeutic behavioral services, the provider is to demonstrate all of the following:
+(1) That it is able to provide all of the following activities of therapeutic behavioral services:
+(a) Collateral contact The provider is able to do both of the following:
+(i) Consult with one or more eligible providers of the services, as specified in paragraph (C)(3) of this rule, for the purpose of addressing an individual's identified needs and participating in service planning. Such consultation may include planning for individualized supports and coordinating healthcare, behavioral healthcare, and non healthcare services in accordance with the individual's comprehensive individualized treatment plan; and
+(ii) Refer an individual to other healthcare, behavioral healthcare and non- healthcare services to avoid more restrictive levels of treatment.
+(b) Use of evidence-based techniques The provider is able to provide solution-focused interventions and emotional and behavioral management for an individual, drawn from evidence-based modalities to ameliorate targeted symptoms and improve the individual's capacity to cope with or prevent symptom manifestation.
+(c) Treatment planning With the individual's participation and consent, the provider is able to utilize strengths-based treatment planning including assisting the individual or individual's support systems (e.g., a parent, guardian, or other treatment professionals involved with the individual's care) in identifying needs and treatment goals associated with the individual's mental illness or behavioral health condition.
+(d) Restoration of self-management skills The provider is able to provide therapeutic interventions designed to promote safe and adaptive alternatives to high-risk, disruptive, and/or self-destructive behaviors resulting in improved emotional and behavioral self-regulation and functioning; improved insight and self-awareness; increased community tenure; and effective functioning to support the individual's ability to maintain safety within the individual's natural environment as related to their diagnosed mental illness or behavioral health condition.
+(e) Crisis prevention and amelioration The provider is able to provide therapeutic support to assist an individual and the individual's support systems to identify potential psychiatric or personal crises, develop crisis management plans, and, as appropriate, provide collateral contact to restore stability and functioning to avoid more restrictive levels of treatment.
+(2) That for each individual to receive the services, it is able to monitor reductions or increases in symptom severity, the need for transition to a higher or lower level of care, and increases or decreases in social skills and daily functioning. Monitoring is needed to detect early signs of clinical deterioration, initiate timely intervention, evaluate response to treatment, adjust the comprehensive individualized treatment plan, and support the individual's recovery goals by strengthening coping skills, community participation, and independence in daily living.
+(3) That it employs or contracts with either or both of the following to provide the services:
+(a) One or more behavioral health practitioners who possess, at a minimum, a bachelor's or master's degree in social work, psychology, nursing, or in a related human services discipline; or
+(b) One or more qualified behavioral health specialists, as defined in rule 5122-29- 30 of the Administrative Code.

Substantive amendment

5122-29-19 — Consultation service

Change detail: Substantive clarification

Consultation is formal, systematic information exchange with a community partner including schools, courts, family members or others who are not the individual's established provider. Needs surveys, consultation documentation and qualified staffing remain.

Likely impact: Agencies should distinguish consultation from payable collateral contacts and document purpose, participant/system and outcome.

Next action: Clinical/billing leads: revise the service decision guide and documentation examples.

⚠️ Risk Flag. The expanded service description does not itself make every consultation Medicaid-reimbursable.

October 7 → October 9 revision: Changes 'individuals' to 'individual' in (C)(1) and adds a period. No substantive October 7-to-revised change identified. Revised PDF pages 47.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective January 1, 2018 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) "Consultation service" means a formal and systematic information exchange between a provider and a person other than a client, which is directed towards the development and improvement of individualized service plans and/or techniques involved in the delivery of behavioral health services.

(B) Consultation may be focused on the clinical condition of a person served by another system or focused on the functioning and dynamics of another system. Consultation related to the clinical condition of a person served shall be provided by staff qualified according to paragraph (C) of this rule.

(1) The provider shall survey periodically other community systems to determine behavioral health consultation needs that may be desired by the systems, persons or families being served by those other systems.

(2) The provider shall maintain a record of all consultation services provided, including the name of the person or system to whom the service was provided, the nature of the consultation, and the outcome of the consultation.

(C) Consultation service shall be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-19 Consultation service.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 47 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A)
As used in this rule, "provider" means a provider described in
rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the
Administrative Code applies.
(B) "Consultation service" means a formal and systematic information exchange between a
provider and a community partner (including, but not limited to, a school system, court,
family member of an individual receiving or to receive behavioral health services, or other
person who is not an established provider of the individual receiving or to receive
behavioral health services) that is directed towards the sharing of information on how to
support, intervene, and address concerns or behaviors of the individual receiving or to
receive behavioral health services, including facilitating the availability of appropriate
behavioral health services for that individual.
(C) Consultation may be focused on the clinical condition of an individual served by
another system or focused on the functioning and dynamics of another system.
Consultation related to the clinical condition of an individual served is to be
provided by staff qualified according to paragraph (D) of this rule.
(1) The provider is to periodically survey other community systems to
determine behavioral health consultation needs that may be desired by the systems,
individual or families being served by those other systems.
(2) The provider is to maintain a record of all consultation services provided,
including the name of the individual or system to whom the service was
provided, the nature of the consultation, and the outcome of the consultation.
(D) Consultation service is to be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified
according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
