Clarification

5122-29-22 — Referral and information service

Change detail: Editorial and applicability clarification

Referral/information requirements retain coordinated information, referral follow-up, accessibility, telephone availability, TRS, contact logs and separation from hotline crisis assistance.

Likely impact: Agencies should maintain referral-access, accessibility, outcome-follow-up and logging controls.

Next action: Operations: refresh references in referral policies and preserve current controls.

⚠️ Risk Flag. The revision removes the redundant parenthetical TRS phrase from (D)(2), but the following standalone paragraph still expressly requires access to telephone communication relay service and describes text-to-speech/signing-to-speech relay.

October 7 → October 9 revision: Deletes redundant parenthetical 'access to telecommunication relay services (TRS)' and adjusts punctuation/conjunctions. The operative standalone TRS requirement remains, so there is no substantive accessibility rollback identified. Revised PDF pages 48–49.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective January 1, 2018 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) "Referral and information service" means responses, usually by telephone, to inquiries from people about services in the community. Referral may include contacting any agency or a provider in order to secure services for the person requesting assistance.

(B) Referral and information service shall be planned and coordinated with other health and human service providers, and shall:

(1) Have a mechanism to compile information about services available in the service system and the community; and

(2) Have mechanisms to determine whether persons referred were able to access services, were satisfied with the services, or experienced any problems with the referral source. This information shall be used to determine if particular providers shall continue to be used as referrals for persons seeking services. All state and federal confidentiality laws shall be adhered to in this process.

(C) The provider shall ensure access and availability of referral and information service including:

(1) A referral and information service shall have a published telephone number, including a published telephone number for special telephone services for the hearing impaired; and

(2) The provider shall ensure access and availability for persons whose primary means of communication is a language other than english, and for persons with communication impairments such as speech, language or hearing disorders, access to telecommunication relay services (TRS), and for persons with visual impairments.

A TRS is a telephone service that allows persons with hearing or speech disabilities to place and receive telephone calls, such services include but are not limited to text to speech relay and signing to speech relay.

(D) Each call and contact shall be logged and shall include the date, time and person answering the call or contact.

(E) A referral and information service is not hotline service, and is not intended to replace the crisis assistance function of a hotline service.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-22 Referral and information service.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 48–49 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A)
As used in this
rule, "provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative
Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
(B) "Referral and information service" means responses, usually by telephone, to inquiries from
individuals about services in the community. Referral may include contacting any agency
or a provider to secure services for the individual requesting assistance.
(C) Referral and information service is to be planned and coordinated with other health
and human service providers and is to satisfy both of the following conditions:
(1) Have a mechanism to compile information about services available in the service
system and community; and
(2) Have processes to determine whether individuals referred were
able to access services, were satisfied with the services, or experienced any
problems with the referral source. This information is to be used to determine if
particular providers are to continue to be used as referrals for
individuals seeking services. All state and federal confidentiality laws
are to be adhered to in this process.
(D) The provider is to ensure access and availability of referral and information service
including satisfying both of the following conditions:
(1) Having a published telephone number for
the referral and information service, including a published telephone number for
special telephone services for the hearing impaired; and
(2) Ensuring access and availability to the referral and
information service for individuals whose primary means of communication
is a language other than English, for persons with communication
impairments (such as speech, language, or hearing disorders
), and for persons with visual impairments.
With respect to individuals with communication impairments, the provider is to
ensure access to a telephone communication relay service (TRS). The services
associated with TRS include, but are not limited to, text to speech relay and signing
to speech relay.
(E) Each call and contact is to be logged and include the date, time, and person
answering the call or contact.
(F) A referral and information service is not a hotline service and is not intended to replace
the crisis assistance function of a hotline service.

The services associated with TRS include, but are not limited to, text to speech relay and signing to speech relay.
+(E) Each call and contact is to be logged and include the date, time, and person answering the call or contact.
+(F) A referral and information service is not a hotline service and is not intended to replace the crisis assistance function of a hotline service.
Substantive amendment

5122-29-27 — Supplemental behavioral health services

Change detail: Substantive certification clarification

Supplemental services remain services outside the chapter's specifically listed services. The revised application clause (C)(1) duplicates the entire (C) application lead-in rather than stating a discrete information item; (C)(2) still expressly requires a supporting ADAMH board letter from each district of intended service. Standards, training and feedback duties remain in (D).

Likely impact: Agencies should continue preparing the service inventory and district-specific board letters, but the posted application checklist needs DBH correction or clarification before relying on it.

Next action: Credentialing/policy leads: request a corrected application checklist and confirmation of the intended service-name/description requirement; continue collecting applicable district ADAMH board support letters.

⚠️ Risk Flag. The p50 PDF visually confirms the duplicate lead-in. It is not an extraction artifact and should not be silently normalized or presented as a settled policy removal of service descriptions. Supplemental certification still does not create Medicaid billing authority or reimbursement.

October 7 → October 9 revision: October 7 (C)(1)'s service-name/brief-description requirement is replaced by a verbatim duplicate of (C)'s application lead-in. Other substantive content is unchanged. Revised PDF pages 50.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective January 1, 2018 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) "Supplemental behavioral health services" means services other than those specifically listed in this chapter. Supplemental behavioral health services may include representative payeeship, outreach, screening, education, and other supportive behavioral health services and may be offered by a variety of entities, including YMCAs, churches, children's cluster or family and children first.

(B) Supplemental behavioral health services approved by the board of alcohol, drug addiction, and mental health services and the department shall:

(1) Ensure that the provider or organization providing the service meets the appropriate standards or regulations under which they operate;

(2) Ensure that staff providing behavioral health services have participated in orientation or training regarding basic information about mental illness, emotional disturbance, and substance use disorders, and know how to obtain assistance from the behavioral health system if needed; and

(3) Develop mechanisms to solicit and receive feedback about the quality of the service from persons served.

(C) Providers furnishing supplemental behavioral health services shall receive certification to provide the service(s) according to either paragraph (A)(1)(f) of rule 5122-25-03 of the Administrative Code or paragraph (A)(1)(f) of rule 5122-25-04 of the Administrative Code.

(A) As used in this rule:
(1) "Mental illness" has the same meaning as in section 5119.01 of the Revised Code.
(2) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code
to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
(B) "Supplemental behavioral health services" means services other than those specifically listed
in this chapter. Supplemental behavioral health services may include representative
payeeship, outreach, screening, education, and other supportive behavioral health services
and may be offered by a variety of entities, including YMCAs, churches, or county family
and children first councils.

services under rule 5122-25-02 or 5122-25-04 of the Administrative Code, or seeking to
add supplemental behavioral health services to the menu of services the provider provides
under rule 5122-25-03 of the Administrative Code, is to include all of the following
information in their application:

health services under rule 5122-25-02 or 5122-25-04 of the Administrative Code, or
seeking to add supplemental behavioral health services to the menu of services the
provider provides under rule 5122-25-03 of the Administrative Code, is to include
all of the following information in their application:

alcohol, drug addiction, and mental health service district where the provider intends
to provide supplemental behavioral health services that describes the board's support
for the provider's provision of the services.

Editorial amendment

5122-29-28 — Intensive home based treatment service

Change detail: Cross-reference and editorial amendment

The proposed IHBT text retains under-21/SED/CANS eligibility, multiple weekly contacts, natural-environment delivery, 24/7 crisis response and supervision, minimum two FTE, six-month average caseload limits, individual training, annual fidelity review/approved EBP pathway, practitioner configurations and FFT-only exception. The proposal changes the telehealth cross-reference in (C)(1) from rule 5122-29-31 to rule 5122-26-22 and moves the fidelity-tool location in (I) from www.medicaid.ohio.gov to ihbtohio.org; both references are unchanged from the October 7 packet.

Likely impact: Agencies should retain substantive IHBT staffing, caseload and eligibility assumptions while updating cross-references and reviewing fidelity resources.

Next action: IHBT lead: verify operative telehealth and fidelity-review resources; policy lead: request consistent agency naming without assuming a transfer of authority.

⚠️ Risk Flag. Revised (I) visibly retains 'Ohio department of mental health and addiction services (OhioMHAS)' as the entity designating an external fidelity reviewer, while nearby proposed approval/certification language changes OhioMHAS to 'the department'. Do not silently rewrite that retained authority text or infer a substantive transfer from inconsistent naming alone.

October 7 → October 9 revision: Compared with the October 7 clean packet's 'designated by the department', revised (I) reads 'designated by the Ohio department of mental health and addiction services (OhioMHAS)' and adds 'a' before department-approved EBP. Remaining diff consists of punctuation/spacing and line-break changes, including 'two,;' cleanup. No new eligibility, staffing, caseload or fidelity thresholds identified. Revised PDF pages 51–56.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective March 1, 2022 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) In addition to the definitions in rule 5122-24-01 of the Administrative Code, the following definitions apply to this rule:

(1) "Caseload" means the individual cases open or assigned to each full-time equivalent IHBT staff.

(2) "Continued stay review" means a review of a child/adolescent's functioning to determine the need for further services to achieve or maintain service goals and objectives.

(3) "Crisis response" means the immediate access and availability, as clinically indicated, to the child/adolescent and family, which may include crisis stabilization services in accordance with rule 5122-29-10 of the Administrative Code, safety planning, and the alleviation of the presenting crisis.

(4) "Family" means any individual or caregiver related by blood or affinity whose close association with the person is the equivalent of a family relationship as identified by the person; including kinship and foster care.

(5) "Home" means any family living arrangement including but not limited to biological, kinship, adoptive, foster home, and non-custodial families who have made a commitment to the child/adolescent.

(6) "Out-of-home placement" means any removal of the child/adolescent from his or her home. Planned respite, where the child's main residence remains their home, is not considered out-of-home placement.

(B) Intensive home based treatment (IHBT) service is a comprehensive behavioral health service provided to a child/adolescent with serious emotional disturbance (SED) and their family, designed to treat mental health conditions that significantly impair functioning. IHBT may also be utilized for the treatment of children and adolescents that have co-occurring substance use or neurodevelopmental needs, when these needs co-occur with a mental health condition. IHBT is provided for the purpose of preventing out of home placement or facilitating a successful transition back home. IHBT integrates trauma-informed and resilience-focused assessment, crisis response, individual and family psychotherapy, service and resource coordination, and rehabilitative skill development with the goal of either preventing the out-of-home placement or facilitating a successful transition back to home. These intensive, time-limited behavioral health services are provided in the child/adolescent's natural environment with the purpose of stabilizing and improving their behavioral health functioning as documented using the Ohio specific child and adolescent needs and strengths (CANS) tool.

The purpose of IHBT is to enable a child/adolescent with SED to function successfully in the least restrictive, most normative environment. IHBT services are culturally, ethnically, racially, developmentally and linguistically appropriate, and respect and build on the strengths of the child/adolescent and family's race, culture, and ethnicity.

(C) The following describes the activities and components of IHBT:

(1) IHBT is an intensive service that consists of multiple in person contacts per week with the child/adolescent and family, which includes collateral contacts related to the behavioral health needs of the child/adolescent as documented in the individual client record (ICR) as required by Chapter 5122-27 of the Administrative Code. IHBT can be provided via telehealth in accordance with rule 5122-29-31 of the Administrative Code.

(2) IHBT is provided in the home, school, and community where the child/adolescent lives and functions;

(3) The frequency and modality of contacts may fluctuate based on the assessed needs and unique circumstances of the child, adolescent, and family;

(4) IHBT is strength-based and family-driven, with both the child/adolescent and family regarded as equal partners with the IHBT staff in all aspects of developing the service plan and service delivery;

(5) Provided by staff with a caseload that averages over any six month period and per full time equivalent staff:

(a) Twelve or less when provided by a team of two, or

(b) Six or less when provided by an individual staff.

(6) Immediate crisis response is available twenty-four hours a day seven days a week by the lead IHBT team member with back-up coverage available from other IHBT team members or the IHBT team supervisor.

(7) Each child/adolescent and family receiving IHBT is assessed for risk and safety issues. A jointly written crisis and safety plan shall be developed that is provided to the child/adolescent and family;

(8) Collaboration is required to be performed with other child-serving agencies or systems, e.g., school, court, developmental disabilities, child welfare, and health care providers that are providing services to the child/adolescent and family, as well as family and community supports identified by the child/adolescent and family;

(9) The service activities and components are individually tailored to meet the needs of the child/adolescent and family. Appointments are made at a time that is convenient to the child/adolescent and family, including evenings and weekends if necessary;

(10) The service is time-limited, with length of stay matched to the presenting behavioral health needs of the child/adolescent and the family; and

(11) The IHBT team will collaboratively develop a plan to transition with each youth and family. The plan will include a focus on transition to other services, supports and providers for services and supports based on the individualized needs of the youth and family.

(D) Eligibility for IHBT will be determined by the IHBT team in collaboration with the youth and family and other cross systems partners by documenting the following criteria:

(1) Is clinically determined to meet the "person with serious emotional disturbance" (SED) criteria in rule 5122-24-01 of the Administrative Code and the child or adolescent;

(a) Is under twenty-one years of age;

(b) Has a mental health need;

(c) Has an Ohio specific CANS assessment that indicates marked to severe behavioral/emotional impairment and at least one of the following:

(i) Impairment that seriously disrupts life functioning; or

(ii) Risk behaviors that are rated as actionable on the CANS.

(2) Meets one or more of the following criteria as documented in the ICR:

(a) Is at risk for out-of-home placement due to their behavioral health conditions;

(b) Has returned within the previous thirty days from an out-of-home placement or is transitioning back to their home within thirty days; or

(c) Requires a high intensity of behavioral health interventions to safely remain in or return home.

(E) The community mental health services or addiction services provider must demonstrate that the following staff requirements and qualifications are met:

(1) A minimum of two full-time equivalent staff provide the service. Services may be provided by a single person, or team of staff clearly sharing various responsibilities for the same child/adolescent and family. Each child/adolescent shall have a staff assigned with lead responsibility.

(2) The provider must have a documented plan for clinical supervision of each team member.

(3) The IHBT supervisor shall have primary responsibility for providing supervision to the IHBT staff twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week. If the IHBT supervisor is unavailable, then supervision must be provided by staff qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.

(F) The provider must demonstrate that each IHBT staff has an individualized training plan based on an assessment of their specific training needs. The following professional training and development criteria must be met:

(1) Each staff receives an assessment of initial training needs based on the skills and competencies necessary to provide IHBT service prior to providing IHBT service; and

(2) The agency shall have a written description of the skills and competencies required to provide IHBT service, which include, at a minimum, the following:

(a) Family systems;

(b) Risk assessment, crisis stabilization, and safety planning;

(c) Parenting skills and supports for children/adolescents with SED;

(d) Cultural competency;

(e) Intersystem collaboration with a focus on schools, courts, and child welfare:

(i) Knowledge of other systems;

(ii) System advocacy; and

(iii) Roles, responsibilities, and mandates of other child/adolescent-serving entities;

(f) Trauma-informed and resiliency-focused care;

(g) Educational and vocational functioning:

(i) Assessment and intervention strategies for resolving barriers to successful educational and vocational functioning;

(ii) Knowledge of special education laws; and

(iii) Strategies for developing positive home-school partnerships and connections;

(h) IHBT philosophy, including strength-based assessment and treatment planning; and

(i) Understanding the complex and interconnected range of symptoms and needs of children and adolescents, including co-occurring substance use disorders and developmental disabilities.

(G) The provider's training plan must include provisions for ongoing training specific to the identified training needs of the staff as it relates to the population served, including attention to cultural competency, changing demographics, new knowledge or research, and other areas identified by the agency.

(H) The provider must demonstrate that each IHBT supervisor receives training specific to the clinical and administrative supervision of the service.

(I) The provider shall obtain satisfactory fidelity reviews based on the provider's specific program modality every twelve months by an individual or organization external to the provider and designated by the Ohio department of mental health and addiction services (OhioMHAS), utilizing the IHBT individual provider model fidelity rating tool, version March 1, 2022 or the IHBT teamed-model fidelity rating tool, Version March 1, 2022 available at www.medicaid.ohio.gov, or be licensed by an OhioMHAS approved evidence-based practice (EBP). The provider shall incorporate the results of the fidelity review into the provider's performance improvement program, if indicated.

(J) Intensive home based treatment service shall be supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.

(K) IHBT shall be provided by persons with competency in the provision of mental health interventions through one of the following program configurations:

(1) At least one licensed practitioner and at least one other licensed practitioner who is authorized to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who are providing an evidence-based practice approved by OhioMHAS and are working in a program licensed by a national accreditation body or their delegate. Each practitioner must have their own caseload of clients.

For those providers who are delivering functional family therapy (FFT), the services may be delivered by an individual who is licensed to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code;

(2) At least two or more licensed or licensed-eligible practitioners who are eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who are providing an evidence-supported practice approved by OhioMHAS. Each practitioner must have their own caseload of clients; or

(3) At least two practitioners eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code. One of the practitioners must be licensed and the other either a qualified behavioral health specialist as defined in rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code or a peer supporter who holds a valid and unrestricted certification from OhioMHAS issued in accordance with rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. The peer supporter must be a family peer supporter or a youth peer supporter in accordance with rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. Peer supporters will also demonstrate competency working with children or adolescents with SED and their families. These practitioners must share a caseload of clients.

(L) A provider of FFT who provides the service in accordance with the national evidence based model, found at https://www.fftllc.com/about-fft-training/clinical-model.html, does not need to meet requirements of paragraphs (C) and (E) to (H) of this rule. Any provider of FFT without meeting all other requirements of this rule will be certified as "IHBT-FFT Only."

according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
(F) The provider is to demonstrate that each IHBT staff has an individualized training plan
based on an assessment of their specific training needs. The following professional
training and development criteria are to be met:
(1) Each staff member receives an assessment of initial training needs based on the skills
and competencies necessary to provide IHBT service prior to providing IHBT
service; and
(2) The agency is to have a written description of the skills and competencies
necessary to provide IHBT service, which include, at a minimum, the
following:
(a) Family systems;
(b) Risk assessment, crisis stabilization, and safety planning;
(c) Parenting skills and supports for children/adolescents with SED;
(d) Cultural competency;
(e) Intersystem collaboration with a focus on schools, courts, and child welfare:
(i) Knowledge of other systems;
(ii) System advocacy; and
(iii) Roles, responsibilities, and mandates of other child/adolescent-serving
entities;
(f) Trauma-informed and resiliency-focused care;
(g) Educational and vocational functioning:
(i) Assessment and intervention strategies for resolving barriers to successful
educational and vocational functioning;
(ii) Knowledge of special education laws; and
(iii) Strategies for developing positive home-school partnerships and
connections;
(h) IHBT philosophy, including strength-based assessment and treatment planning;
and
(i) Understanding the complex and interconnected range of symptoms and needs of
children and adolescents, including co-occurring substance use disorders and
developmental disabilities.
(G) The provider's training plan is to include provisions for ongoing training specific to the
identified training needs of the staff as it relates to the population served, including
attention to cultural competency, changing demographics, new knowledge or research,
and other areas identified by the agency.
(H) The provider is to demonstrate that each IHBT supervisor receives training specific to
the clinical and administrative supervision of the service.
(I) The provider is to obtain satisfactory fidelity reviews based on the provider's specific
program modality every twelve months by an individual or organization external to the
provider and designated by the Ohio department of mental health and addiction services
(OhioMHAS), utilizing the IHBT individual provider model fidelity rating tool, version
March 1, 2022, or the IHBT teamed-model fidelity rating tool, version March 1,
2022, available at ihbtohio.org, or be licensed by
a department-approved evidence-based practice (EBP). The provider is to
incorporate the results of the fidelity review into the provider's performance improvement
program, if indicated.
(J) Intensive home based treatment service is to be supervised by staff who are qualified
according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
(K) IHBT is to be provided by persons with competency in the provision of mental health
interventions through one of the following program configurations:
(1) At least one licensed practitioner and at least one other licensed practitioner who is
authorized to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative
Code and who are providing an evidence-based practice approved by
the department and are working in a program licensed by a national
accreditation body or their delegate. Each practitioner is to have their own
caseload of clients.
For those providers who are delivering functional family therapy (FFT), the services
may be delivered by an individual who is licensed to provide services pursuant to
rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code;
(2) At least two or more licensed or licensed-eligible practitioners who are eligible to
provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who
are providing an evidence-supported practice approved by the
department. Each practitioner must have their own caseload of clients; or
(3) At least two practitioners eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of
the Administrative Code. One of the practitioners is to be licensed and the other
either a qualified behavioral health specialist as defined in rule 5122-29-30 of the
Administrative Code or a peer supporter who holds a valid and unrestricted
certification from the department issued in accordance with rule 5122-
29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. The peer supporter is to be a family peer
supporter or a youth peer supporter in accordance with rule 5122-29-15.1 of the
Administrative Code. Peer supporters will also demonstrate competency working
with children or adolescents with SED and their families. These practitioners
are to share a caseload of clients.
(L) A provider of FFT who provides the service in accordance with the national evidence based
model, found at https://www.fftllc.com/about-fft-training/clinical-model.html, does not
need to meet the standards of paragraphs (C) and (E) to (H) of this rule. Any
provider of FFT without meeting all other standards of this rule will be
certified as "IHBT-FFT Only."

 (A) In addition to the definitions in rule 5122-24-01 of the Administrative Code, the following definitions apply to this rule:

 (3) "Crisis response" means the immediate access and availability, as clinically indicated, to the child/adolescent and family, which may include crisis stabilization services in accordance with rule 5122-29-10 of the Administrative Code, safety planning, and the alleviation of the presenting crisis.

-(6) "Out-of-home placement" means any removal of the child/adolescent from his or her home. Planned respite, where the child's main residence remains their home, is not considered out-of-home placement.
-(B) Intensive home based treatment (IHBT) service is a comprehensive behavioral health service provided to a child/adolescent with serious emotional disturbance (SED) and their family, designed to treat mental health conditions that significantly impair functioning. IHBT may also be utilized for the treatment of children and adolescents that have co-occurring substance use or neurodevelopmental needs, when these needs co-occur with a mental health condition. IHBT is provided for the purpose of preventing out of home placement or facilitating a successful transition back home. IHBT integrates trauma-informed and resilience-focused assessment, crisis response, individual and family psychotherapy, service and resource coordination, and rehabilitative skill development with the goal of either preventing the out-of-home placement or facilitating a successful transition back to home. These intensive, time-limited behavioral health services are provided in the child/adolescent's natural environment with the purpose of stabilizing and improving their behavioral health functioning as documented using the Ohio specific child and adolescent needs and strengths (CANS) tool. The purpose of IHBT is to enable a child/adolescent with SED to function successfully in the least restrictive, most normative environment. IHBT services are culturally, ethnically, racially, developmentally and linguistically appropriate, and respect and build on the strengths of the child/adolescent and family's race, culture, and ethnicity.

-(G)  The provider's training plan is to include provisions for ongoing training specific to the identified training needs of the staff as it relates to the population served, including attention to cultural competency, changing demographics, new knowledge or research, and other areas identified by the agency.
-(H)  The provider is to demonstrate that each IHBT supervisor receives training specific to the clinical and administrative supervision of the service.
-(I)  The provider is to obtain satisfactory fidelity reviews based on the provider's specific program modality every twelve months by an individual or organization external to the provider and designated by the department, utilizing the IHBT individual provider model fidelity rating tool, version March 1, 2022, or the IHBT teamed-model fidelity rating tool, version March 1, 2022, available at ihbtohio.org, or be licensed by department-approved evidence-based practice (EBP). The provider is to incorporate the results of the fidelity review into the provider's performance improvement program, if indicated.
-(J)  Intensive home based treatment service is to be supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
-(K)  IHBT is to be provided by persons with competency in the provision of mental health interventions through one of the following program configurations:
-(1)  At least one licensed practitioner and at least one other licensed practitioner who is authorized to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who are providing an evidence-based practice approved by the department and are working in a program licensed by a national accreditation body or their delegate. Each practitioner is to have their own caseload of clients. For those providers who are delivering functional family therapy (FFT), the services may be delivered by an individual who is licensed to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code;
-(2)  At least two or more licensed or licensed-eligible practitioners who are eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who are providing an evidence-supported practice approved by the department. Each practitioner must have their own caseload of clients; or
-(3)  At least two practitioners eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code. One of the practitioners is to be licensed and the other either a qualified behavioral health specialist as defined in rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code or a peer supporter who holds a valid and unrestricted certification from the department issued in accordance with rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. The peer supporter is to be a family peer supporter or a youth peer supporter in accordance with rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. Peer supporters will also demonstrate competency working with children or adolescents with SED and their families. These practitioners are to share a caseload of clients.
-(L)  A provider of FFT who provides the service in accordance with the national evidence based model, found at https://www.fftllc.com/about-fft-training/clinical-model.html, does not need to meet the standards of paragraphs (C) and (E) to (H) of this rule. Any provider of FFT without meeting all other standards of this rule will be certified as "IHBT-FFT Only."
+(A) In addition to the definitions in rule 5122-24-01 of the Administrative Code, the following definitions apply to this rule:
+(1) "Caseload" means the individual cases open or assigned to each full-time equivalent IHBT staff member.
+(2) "Continued stay review" means a review of a child/adolescent's functioning to determine the need for further services to achieve or maintain service goals and objectives.
+(3) "Crisis response" means the immediate access and availability, as clinically indicated, to the child/adolescent and family, which may include crisis stabilization services in accordance with rule 5122-29-10 of the Administrative Code, safety planning, and the alleviation of the presenting crisis.
+(4) "Family" means any individual or caregiver related by blood or affinity whose close association with the child/adolescent is the equivalent of a family relationship as identified by the child/adolescent, including kinship and foster care.
+(5) "Home" means any family living arrangement including but not limited to biological, kinship, adoptive, foster home, and non-custodial families who have made a commitment to the child/adolescent.
+(6) "Out-of-home placement" means any removal of the child/adolescent from his or her home. Planned respite, where the child's or adolescent's main residence remains their home, is not considered out-of-home placement.
+(7) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
+(B) Intensive home based treatment (IHBT) service is a comprehensive behavioral health service provided to a child/adolescent with serious emotional disturbance (SED) and their family, designed to treat mental health conditions that significantly impair functioning. IHBT may also be utilized for the treatment of children and adolescents that have co- occurring substance use or neurodevelopmental needs, when these needs co-occur with a mental health condition. IHBT is provided for the purpose of preventing out of home placement or facilitating a successful transition back home. IHBT integrates trauma- informed and resilience-focused assessment, crisis response, individual and family psychotherapy, service and resource coordination, and rehabilitative skill development with the goal of either preventing the out-of-home placement or facilitating a successful transition back to home. These intensive, time-limited behavioral health services are provided in the child/adolescent's natural environment with the purpose of stabilizing and improving their behavioral health functioning as documented using the Ohio specific child and adolescent needs and strengths (CANS) tool. The purpose of IHBT is to enable a child/adolescent with SED to function successfully in the least restrictive, most normative environment. IHBT services are culturally, ethnically, racially, developmentally and linguistically appropriate, and respect and build on the strengths of the child/adolescent and family's race, culture, and ethnicity.
+(C) The following describes the activities and components of IHBT:
+(1) IHBT is an intensive service that consists of multiple in person contacts per week with the child/adolescent and family, which includes collateral contacts related to the behavioral health needs of the child/adolescent as documented in the individual client record (ICR) as mandated by Chapter 5122-27 of the Administrative Code. IHBT can be provided via telehealth in accordance with rule 5122-26-22 of the Administrative Code.
+(2) IHBT is provided in the home, school, and community where the child/adolescent lives and functions;
+(3) The frequency and modality of contacts may fluctuate based on the assessed needs and unique circumstances of the child, adolescent, and family;
+(4) IHBT is strength-based and family-driven, with both the child/adolescent and family regarded as equal partners with the IHBT staff in all aspects of developing the service plan and service delivery;
+(5) IHBT is provided by staff with a caseload that averages over any six month period and per full time equivalent staff:
+(a) Twelve or less when provided by a team of two ; or
+(b) Six or less when provided by an individual staff member.
+(6) Immediate crisis response is available twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week by the lead IHBT team member with back-up coverage available from other IHBT team members or the IHBT team supervisor.
+(7) Each child/adolescent and family receiving IHBT is assessed for risk and safety issues. A jointly written crisis and safety plan is to be developed and provided to the child/adolescent and family .
+(8) Collaboration is mandated to be performed with other child-serving agencies or systems, e.g., school, court, developmental disabilities, child welfare, and health care providers that are providing services to the child/adolescent and family, as well as family and community supports identified by the child/adolescent and family .
+(9) The service activities and components are individually tailored to meet the needs of the child/adolescent and family. Appointments are made at a time that is convenient to the child/adolescent and family, including evenings and weekends if necessary .
+(10) The service is time-limited, with length of stay matched to the presenting behavioral health needs of the child/adolescent and the family .
+(11) The IHBT team will collaboratively develop a plan to transition with each youth and family. The plan will include a focus on transition to other services, supports and providers for services and supports based on the individualized needs of the youth and family.
+(D) Eligibility for IHBT will be determined by the IHBT team in collaboration with the youth and family and other cross systems partners by documenting the following criteria:
+(1) Is clinically determined to meet the " individual with serious emotional disturbance" (SED) criteria in rule 5122-24-01 of the Administrative Code and the child or adolescent;
+(a) Is under twenty-one years of age;
+(b) Has a mental health need;
+(c) Has an Ohio specific CANS assessment that indicates marked to severe behavioral/emotional impairment and at least one of the following:
+(i) Impairment that seriously disrupts life functioning; or
+(ii) Risk behaviors that are rated as actionable on the CANS.
+(2) Meets one or more of the following criteria as documented in the ICR:
+(a) Is at risk for out-of-home placement due to their behavioral health conditions;
+(b) Has returned within the previous thirty days from an out-of-home placement or is transitioning back to their home within thirty days; or
+(c) Needs a high intensity of behavioral health interventions to safely remain in or return home.
+(E) The provider is to demonstrate that the following staff standards and qualifications are met:
+(1) A minimum of two full-time equivalent staff members provide the service. Services may be provided by a single individual or team of staff clearly sharing various responsibilities for the same child/adolescent and family. Each child/adolescent is to have a staff assigned with lead responsibility.
+(2) The provider is to have a documented plan for clinical supervision of each team member.
+(3) The IHBT supervisor is to have primary responsibility for providing supervision to the IHBT staff twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week. If the IHBT supervisor is unavailable, then supervision is to be provided by staff qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
+(F) The provider is to demonstrate that each IHBT staff has an individualized training plan based on an assessment of their specific training needs. The following professional training and development criteria are to be met:
+(1) Each staff member receives an assessment of initial training needs based on the skills and competencies necessary to provide IHBT service prior to providing IHBT service; and
+(2) The agency is to have a written description of the skills and competencies necessary to provide IHBT service, which include, at a minimum, the following:
+(a) Family systems;
+(b) Risk assessment, crisis stabilization, and safety planning;
+(c) Parenting skills and supports for children/adolescents with SED;
+(d) Cultural competency;
+(e) Intersystem collaboration with a focus on schools, courts, and child welfare:
+(i) Knowledge of other systems;
+(ii) System advocacy; and (iii) Roles, responsibilities, and mandates of other child/adolescent-serving entities;
+(f) Trauma-informed and resiliency-focused care;
+(g) Educational and vocational functioning:
+(i) Assessment and intervention strategies for resolving barriers to successful educational and vocational functioning;
+(ii) Knowledge of special education laws; and (iii) Strategies for developing positive home-school partnerships and connections;
+(h) IHBT philosophy, including strength-based assessment and treatment planning; and
+(i) Understanding the complex and interconnected range of symptoms and needs of children and adolescents, including co-occurring substance use disorders and developmental disabilities.
+(G) The provider's training plan is to include provisions for ongoing training specific to the identified training needs of the staff as it relates to the population served, including attention to cultural competency, changing demographics, new knowledge or research, and other areas identified by the agency.
+(H) The provider is to demonstrate that each IHBT supervisor receives training specific to the clinical and administrative supervision of the service.
+(I) The provider is to obtain satisfactory fidelity reviews based on the provider's specific program modality every twelve months by an individual or organization external to the provider and designated by the Ohio department of mental health and addiction services (OhioMHAS), utilizing the IHBT individual provider model fidelity rating tool, version March 1, 2022, or the IHBT teamed-model fidelity rating tool, version March 1, 2022, available at ihbtohio.org, or be licensed by a department-approved evidence-based practice (EBP). The provider is to incorporate the results of the fidelity review into the provider's performance improvement program, if indicated.
+(J) Intensive home based treatment service is to be supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.
+(K) IHBT is to be provided by persons with competency in the provision of mental health interventions through one of the following program configurations:
+(1) At least one licensed practitioner and at least one other licensed practitioner who is authorized to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who are providing an evidence-based practice approved by the department and are working in a program licensed by a national accreditation body or their delegate. Each practitioner is to have their own caseload of clients. For those providers who are delivering functional family therapy (FFT), the services may be delivered by an individual who is licensed to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code;
+(2) At least two or more licensed or licensed-eligible practitioners who are eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and who are providing an evidence-supported practice approved by the department. Each practitioner must have their own caseload of clients; or
+(3) At least two practitioners eligible to provide services pursuant to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code. One of the practitioners is to be licensed and the other either a qualified behavioral health specialist as defined in rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code or a peer supporter who holds a valid and unrestricted certification from the department issued in accordance with rule 5122- 29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. The peer supporter is to be a family peer supporter or a youth peer supporter in accordance with rule 5122-29-15.1 of the Administrative Code. Peer supporters will also demonstrate competency working with children or adolescents with SED and their families. These practitioners are to share a caseload of clients.
+(L) A provider of FFT who provides the service in accordance with the national evidence based model, found at https://www.fftllc.com/about-fft-training/clinical-model.html, does not need to meet the standards of paragraphs (C) and (E) to (H) of this rule. Any provider of FFT without meeting all other standards of this rule will be certified as "IHBT-FFT Only."
Substantive amendment

5122-29-29 — Assertive community treatment services

Proposed ACT retains severe/persistent mental illness with high service intensity, co-occurring diagnoses, 24/7 availability, initial 3.0 fidelity, annual team reviews and 4.0 within three years. Subsequent fidelity failure requires waiver or team disbandment; waiver evidence includes a fidelity plan and internal steering committee tied to quality improvement. Outreach-contact requirements and qualified staffing remain.

Likely impact: Agencies should track team fidelity deadlines, remediation and waiver readiness; retain the existing distinction between certification requirements and ODM payment criteria.

Next action: ACT program/quality leads: inventory fidelity scores and prepare a waiver remediation workflow.

⚠️ Risk Flag. DBH certification requirements alone do not establish ACT claim eligibility. The proposed source retains 'is do one of the following' in (G), a grammatical drafting defect.

October 7 → October 9 revision: No text change identified between October 7 clean proposed packet and revised proposed reading. Revised PDF pages 57–58.

Full current and proposed rule text

Current effective rule

Effective November 21, 2020 · verified October 13, 2026

Official current text
(A) Assertive community treatment (ACT) services are provided to an individual with a major functional impairment or behavior which present a high risk to the individual due to severe and persistent mental illness and which necessitate high service intensity. ACT services are also provided to the individual's family and other support systems. A client receiving ACT services may also have coexisting substance use disorder, physical health diagnoses, and/or mild intellectual disability. The service is available twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week.

(B) The purpose of ACT team services is to provide the necessary services and supports which maximize recovery, and promote success in employment, housing, and the community.

(C) ACT service providers shall employ one or more teams of practitioners which meet the minimum fidelity criteria as described in paragraphs (D) and (E) of this rule using the tool for measurement of ACT (TMACT) or dartmouth assertive community treatment scale (DACTS).

(D) For initial certification, each ACT team must achieve a minimum average overall fidelity score of 3.0 as determined by an independent validation entity recognized by the department. At its discretion, the Ohio department of mental health and addiction services (OhioMHAS) may lower the minimum score due to the COVID-19 state of emergency declared by the governor.

(E) For continuing certification, each ACT team must achieve and maintain a minimum average overall fidelity score of 4.0 within three years of initial certification as determined by an independent validation entity recognized by the department. At its discretion, OhioMHAS may lower the minimum score due to the COVID-19 state of aemergency declared by the governor.

(F) ACT teams shall have regular repeat fidelity reviews, no more than twelve months from the report date of the previous fidelity review, by an independent validation entity recognized by the department.

(G) At any time after certification of the ACT service, the department may request a new fidelity review based on specific findings of non-compliance with the rules in this chapter.

(H) For a minimum of ninety days, or until the client has stated their desire to discontinue ACT services, the ACT team shall attempt at least two contacts per month for a client who has discontinued ACT services unexpectedly. Such attempts and client response, if any, shall be documented in the individual client record.

(I) ACT shall be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code.

Revised DBH proposal — October 9 redline

PDF rule heading: 5122-29-29 Assertive community treatment (ACT) services.

58-page redline retrieved October 13, 2026 · PDF pages 57–58 · draft, no effective date

Reviewed redline PDF · DBH-hosted source

(A) As used in this rule:
(1) "ACT certified provider" means a provider that is certified under this rule to provide
assertive community treatment services.
(2) "ACT team" means a multidisciplinary team of practitioners, all of whom are
qualified in accordance with rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and have
specialty training in the provision of assertive community treatment, who provide
team-based services in accordance with the ACT model and are employees or
contractors of an ACT certified provider.
(3) "Approved fidelity review" means a fidelity review conducted by an independent
validation entity recognized by the department.
(4) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code
to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
(B) Assertive community treatment (ACT) services are intended for individuals diagnosed with
a severe and persistent mental illness that need high service intensity. ACT services are
also provided to the family members and other support systems of such individuals.
Individuals receiving ACT services may also have a co-occurring diagnosis.
(C) The purpose of ACT services is to provide the necessary interventions and support to
maximize recovery and promote success in employment, housing, and stability in the
community. The service is available twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week.
(D) ACT services providers are to employ one or more ACT teams to implement one or more
programs that meet the minimum fidelity criteria described in this rule using the tool for
measurement of assertive community treatment (TMACT) or the dartmouth assertive
community treatment scale (DACTS).
(E) Before submitting an application for initial certification under this rule, a provider is to
undergo an approved fidelity review and achieve a minimum fidelity score of 3.0.
(F) Once a provider becomes an ACT certified provider, each of its ACT teams is to undergo
ongoing approved fidelity reviews. Each fidelity review is to occur within twelve months
of the team's most recent fidelity review.
(G) Within three years of each ACT team's initial approved fidelity review, the team is to
achieve a minimum fidelity score of 4.0. If a team fails to achieve that standard or fails to
achieve a minimum fidelity score of 4.0 at any fidelity review conducted after the three-
year period, the provider responsible for the ACT team is do one of the following:
(1) Submit a waiver request to the department in accordance with rule 5122-25-10 of the
Administrative Code; or
(2) Disband the ACT team because of fidelity noncompliance.
(H) As part of a waiver request submitted under paragraph (G)(1) of this rule, the provider
responsible for the ACT team is to submit all of the following:
(1) A copy of the ACT team's most recent fidelity review report.
(2) A proposed timeframe by which the ACT team will achieve the minimum 4.0 fidelity
score.
(3) A fidelity action plan the ACT team has developed in collaboration with an
independent validation entity recognized by the department that addresses all
problem areas identified in the most recent fidelity review report.
(4) Documentation that the provider has established an internal steering committee
responsible for guiding and monitoring the implementation of the fidelity action
plan described in paragraph (H)(3) of this rule. The committee is to include, at a
minimum, the provider's ACT team leader, clinical director, and quality
improvement or quality assurance director.
(5) If at the time the waiver is requested the ACT team is subject to a previously-granted
waiver, documentation that that ACT team has undergone a new fidelity review
prior to the expiration of the previously-issued waiver and a copy of the results of
that review.
(I) If an ACT team has developed a fidelity action plan described in paragraph (H)(3) of this
rule, the provider responsible for the ACT team is to (a) ensure that plan is addressed in
the provider's quality improvement plan developed under rule 5122-28-03 of the
Administrative Code and (b) monitor progress toward achieving plan objectives.
(J) The department may request that a certified ACT provider undergo a new fidelity review at
any time based on one or more findings of noncompliance with this chapter.
(K) Prior to termination of ACT services due to an individual's unexpected non-participation, an
ACT team will attempt at least two contacts per month with the individual until one of the
following is the case:
(1) The ACT team has attempted to contact the individual on at least two occasions each
month for a period of ninety days and the individual has not responded. An ACT
team is to document each attempt at contacting an individual in the individual's
record; or
(2) The individual notifies the ACT team of the individual's desire to discontinue the
services.
(L) ACT services are to be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule
5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code. A particular staff member may provide only
those components of the service for which the individual possesses the appropriate
qualifications or credential.

The committee is to include, at a minimum, the provider's ACT team leader, clinical director, and quality improvement or quality assurance director.
-(5)  If at the time the waiver is requested the ACT team is subject to a previously-granted waiver, documentation that that ACT team has undergone a new fidelity review prior to the expiration of the previously-issued waiver and a copy of the results of that review.
-(I)  If an ACT team has developed a fidelity action plan described in paragraph (H)(3) of this rule, the provider responsible for the ACT team is to (a) ensure that plan is addressed in the provider's quality improvement plan developed under rule 5122-28-03 of the Administrative Code and (b) monitor progress toward achieving plan objectives.
-(J)  The department may request that a certified ACT provider undergo a new fidelity review at any time based on one or more findings of noncompliance with this chapter.
-(K)  Prior to termination of ACT services due to an individual's unexpected non-participation, an ACT team will attempt at least two contacts per month with the individual until one of the following is the case:
-(1)  The ACT team has attempted to contact the individual on at least two occasions each month for a period of ninety days and the individual has not responded. An ACT team is to document each attempt at contacting an individual in the individual's record; or
-(2)  The individual notifies the ACT team of the individual's desire to discontinue the services.
-(L)  ACT services are to be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code. A particular staff member may provide only those components of the service for which the individual possesses the appropriate qualifications or credential.
+(A) As used in this rule:
+(1) "ACT certified provider" means a provider that is certified under this rule to provide assertive community treatment services.
+(2) "ACT team" means a multidisciplinary team of practitioners, all of whom are qualified in accordance with rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code and have specialty training in the provision of assertive community treatment, who provide team-based services in accordance with the ACT model and are employees or contractors of an ACT certified provider.
+(3) "Approved fidelity review" means a fidelity review conducted by an independent validation entity recognized by the department.
+(4) "Provider" means a provider described in rule 5122-25-01 of the Administrative Code to whom Chapter 5122-25 of the Administrative Code applies.
+(B) Assertive community treatment (ACT) services are intended for individuals diagnosed with a severe and persistent mental illness that need high service intensity. ACT services are also provided to the family members and other support systems of such individuals. Individuals receiving ACT services may also have a co-occurring diagnosis.
+(C) The purpose of ACT services is to provide the necessary interventions and support to maximize recovery and promote success in employment, housing, and stability in the community. The service is available twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week.
+(D) ACT services providers are to employ one or more ACT teams to implement one or more programs that meet the minimum fidelity criteria described in this rule using the tool for measurement of assertive community treatment (TMACT) or the dartmouth assertive community treatment scale (DACTS).
+(E) Before submitting an application for initial certification under this rule, a provider is to undergo an approved fidelity review and achieve a minimum fidelity score of 3.0.
+(F) Once a provider becomes an ACT certified provider, each of its ACT teams is to undergo ongoing approved fidelity reviews. Each fidelity review is to occur within twelve months of the team's most recent fidelity review.
+(G) Within three years of each ACT team's initial approved fidelity review, the team is to achieve a minimum fidelity score of 4.0. If a team fails to achieve that standard or fails to achieve a minimum fidelity score of 4.0 at any fidelity review conducted after the three- year period, the provider responsible for the ACT team is do one of the following:
+(1) Submit a waiver request to the department in accordance with rule 5122-25-10 of the Administrative Code; or
+(2) Disband the ACT team because of fidelity noncompliance.
+(H) As part of a waiver request submitted under paragraph (G)(1) of this rule, the provider responsible for the ACT team is to submit all of the following:
+(1) A copy of the ACT team's most recent fidelity review report.
+(2) A proposed timeframe by which the ACT team will achieve the minimum 4.0 fidelity score.
+(3) A fidelity action plan the ACT team has developed in collaboration with an independent validation entity recognized by the department that addresses all problem areas identified in the most recent fidelity review report.
+(4) Documentation that the provider has established an internal steering committee responsible for guiding and monitoring the implementation of the fidelity action plan described in paragraph (H)(3) of this rule. The committee is to include, at a minimum, the provider's ACT team leader, clinical director, and quality improvement or quality assurance director.
+(5) If at the time the waiver is requested the ACT team is subject to a previously-granted waiver, documentation that that ACT team has undergone a new fidelity review prior to the expiration of the previously-issued waiver and a copy of the results of that review.
+(I) If an ACT team has developed a fidelity action plan described in paragraph (H)(3) of this rule, the provider responsible for the ACT team is to (a) ensure that plan is addressed in the provider's quality improvement plan developed under rule 5122-28-03 of the Administrative Code and (b) monitor progress toward achieving plan objectives.
+(J) The department may request that a certified ACT provider undergo a new fidelity review at any time based on one or more findings of noncompliance with this chapter.
+(K) Prior to termination of ACT services due to an individual's unexpected non-participation, an ACT team will attempt at least two contacts per month with the individual until one of the following is the case:
+(1) The ACT team has attempted to contact the individual on at least two occasions each month for a period of ninety days and the individual has not responded. An ACT team is to document each attempt at contacting an individual in the individual's record; or
+(2) The individual notifies the ACT team of the individual's desire to discontinue the services.
+(L) ACT services are to be provided and supervised by staff who are qualified according to rule 5122-29-30 of the Administrative Code. A particular staff member may provide only those components of the service for which the individual possesses the appropriate qualifications or credential.

Package revision, BIA review and operational actions

October 7 → October 9 review — all 18 rules

Minor punctuation and wording edits are distinguished from population, paragraph-structure and source-drafting changes. The page references below refer to the reviewed 58-page redline.

RuleRevised PDF pagesChange from October 7 clean draft
5122-29-011–1No text change.
5122-29-032–4General services definition moves from (B) to (A)(1); agency/provider definition is now (A)(2). Credential/ASAM requirements move from (C) to (B), assessments from (D) to (C), counseling from (E) to (D), and medical activities from (F) to (E). Assessment definition adds 'and/or' before tobacco/nicotine. This is reorganization and clarification rather than a new October 9 service model.

DBH draft-rule webpageThe supplied page returned 404 during this review; both official PDF links remained accessible.The site retains the previously announced October 14, 5 p.m. Eastern deadline. Confirm the latest submission instructions before sending comments.

BIA review

The unchanged BIA lists the same 18 rules. Questions 9–10 describe stakeholder discussions, the September comment period and the October 6 ODM meeting. DBH reports restoring recovery/resiliency/wellness wording, restoring SUD case-management references, removing ambiguous crisis references and “skilled treatment,” adjusting peer-run hours/funding, and clarifying ACT co-occurring eligibility. These narrative statements do not replace the rule text.

⚠️ Risk Flag. The BIA mentions prescriber availability when medication management is offered, but revised 5122-29-06 contains no express prescriber-availability clause. It also mentions group SUD case management, which revised 5122-29-13 does not expressly state. Obtain clarification before converting either statement into a billing or forecasting rule.

BIA question 15 acknowledges certification fees and employee compliance time without quantifying agency counts, hours, staffing costs or dollars. Its “new service” language differs from the inventory of 18 amended rules. Question 10 discusses burden reduction while question 16 answers “No.” Question 14 promises education after final filing without an implementation date. These are unresolved analysis questions, not evidence that financial impact is zero.

Operational actions

  • Clinical and billing leads should crosswalk actual CPST/TBS/PSR activities, treatment-plan purpose and member needs against both certification and payment criteria.
  • Credentialing should map DBH degree/QBHS and ODM degree/experience requirements independently, obtain the proposed Appendix A, and identify the permitted activity tier for each PSR rendering provider.
  • Agency leadership should separate peer-run public funding from reimbursable peer services and evaluate attendance, staffing, documentation and ACT remediation costs.
  • Policy leads should request correction of the PSR title/duplicate paragraphs, supplemental-services application item, general-services chapter reference, QRTP duplication and DIP retention overlap.

DBH certification does not independently establish ODM payment. MCO, OhioRISE and MyCare authorization, claims edits and payment implementation remain payer-specific. No draft-based change to production claims or current service eligibility is authorized by this analysis.

Source errors and dependencies

Confirmed source issues include 5122-29-03(A)(2)’s “Chapter 5122-25-01”; forensic referral wording at -07(C)(3); the QRTP service-name inconsistency and duplicated agency wording at -09.1(C)(3)(a); crisis punctuation/CPR scope at -10; DIP six-/seven-year retention overlap and incomplete curriculum phrases; peer-support “in accordance with in rule” and “employeed”; repeated PSR (D)/(E); supplemental-services (C)(1); IHBT’s retained OhioMHAS reference; and ACT’s “is do.” Corrected October 7 typos are no longer described as open defects. The proposed Appendix A remains absent.

Review and source integrity

All 58 revised-rule pages and six BIA pages were reviewed. Deleted red text and strike-through geometry were excluded from the proposed-text reading; black retained and blue added text were preserved. Full proposed panels retain duplicated or defective wording rather than silently repairing it. Representative deletion, TBS, PSR, QRTP, supplemental-services and IHBT pages were visually inspected. The displayed diffs reflow paragraph wrapping, and a separate revision diff preserves the October 7 baseline. Official PDF links and frozen reviewed PDF copies allow readers to verify the source directly.
